PLEASANT PARAGRAPHS.

THE saddest of all songs is that of the collector, "A due, kind friends, a due." DEBTOR: I want to pay that little bill of yours. Creditor (delighted): All right, my dear boy. Debtor: But I can't. TB.AMP (approaching from behind): I ain't 'ad bite to-day, Captain. Enthusiastic Angler: Wrong bait, I expect. Try worms. "WHY weren't you here yesterday to do the job I gave you, John?' "Very sorry, sir, but I had to preside at a meeting of the unemployed." "PA, have dogs got wings?" "No. child ; don't you know better than that?" "Why, pa, this paper says a big dog flew at a man and bit him. THE FACTS OF IT.—Smuff: "You and Jinks don't seem to be as thick as you were. Does he owe you money?" Bluff: "No, not exactly; but lie wants to." SCBTRACTION ILLUSTRATED.—Teacher to little Tommy: Now, Tommy, suppose you gave your little brother nine apples and took back seven, what would that make? Tommy: It would make him yell. "My son," said his father solemnly, "when you see a boy loafing about the street corners at all hours, what place in life do you suppose he is fitting himself for?" "To be a policeman," replied the young philosopher promptly. Au= EvEs.—Papa : See that spider, my boy, spinning his web. Is it not wonderful? DJ you reflect that, try as he may, no man could spin that web? Johnny: What of it? See me spin this top? Do you reflect that, try as he may, no spider cuuid pin this top? " WHY do you call yours a parlour ?" said the patient to the dentist. "What would you call it?" "Well, I should fancy drawing-rooin would -be more appropriate." PHIL.ANTHROPIST : What would you do with a copper if I gave you one? Tramp sarcastically : Git a new coat, mister, an' some supper an' a night's lodgin', an' breakfast an' dinner ter-morrer. Philanthropist: Here, take this sixpence, and suppert,yourself for the rest uf your life." A SArE BET.—" Is your mamma in ?" "What's your name?" "I'm Mr. Spurgeon, the new minister." "Then I think mamma's expectin' you —'cause she said she'd bet a shillin' you'd be sure to come on washing day!" PAPA, dear, why are these waterproof soles called gutta percha?'" "Because, my lad, they enable you to perch in the gutter without getting wet." BOARDER: The chicken soup seems to be rather weak. Landlady: I don't see why. I told the cook how to make it, tilt perhaps she didn't catch the idea. Boarder: Perhaps she didn't catch the chicken.

"ARE you fond of flowers ?" he said. "Yes." she answered, and her eyes brightened as she fore- saw a beautiful big bunch oi roses. "Suppose, then," he said, "that we take a walk in the park. There are some lovely flower beds there." LITTLE ETHEL : Pans, you ought to have the doctor, come and see ioheay. lam 'fraid he's ill. Papa: Why, he's out in the street playing, and yelling like a wild Indian. Little Ethel: Yes, but when he is well he yells like two wild Indians. "GRANDPA, what is a peacemaker?" "Well, a peacemaker is a man who jumps in to prevent two other men from fighting, and comes out with more bruises and black eyes than either of them." "JOHNNY," said his teacher to the wise boy who not only knew his father but also his uncle, "if your father can do a piece of work in seven days, and your uncle George can do it in nine days, how long would it take both of them to do it?" "They'd never get it done," said Johnny. "They'd sit down and discuss politics." Suseicsose—" Do you remember that girl who came here and said that what she most desired was a good home?" asked the housewife. "What ,is the matter now?" responded her husband. Have you missed something else?" "Yes. I guess she has a good home pretty nearly paid .or by this time." BOARDER warmly: "Oh, I'm knowing to the tricks of your trade. Do you think I have lived, Lin boarding-houses twenty years for nothing?" Landlady (frigidly): "I shouldn't be at all surprised." "YES," said the delegate. "I said I was the working-man's friend." "But you don't do any -work," suggested the man with the horny hands. "No; not at present." "And you never did any work." "That's true. You see, what the work- ' ing-man most needs is work, and I am too much , the working-man's friend to run any risk of taking work away from him." ' WELL LOOKED AFTER.—" Brooks," said Rivers, "you ought to do something for that cold of yours. A neglected cold sometimes leads to serious conse- quences." "This cold of mine isn't neglected," crossly answered Brooks. "Five or six hundred of my friends are looking after it." "THIS is the sixth consecutive day we've had chicken," said Mrs. Brown while in the country. "Don't complain," replied her husband; "just think that every slay there's one cackler loss to wake us up in the morning at sunrise." TEACHER : "Way didn't your brother come to school to-day, Johnny?" Johnny: "He hurt his foot this morning so he could hardly walk." Teacher: "That's a lame excuse, but I suppose I must accept it." ACCLMACY.—" Now, Ethel, tell me how many bones you have in your body?" Ethel: "Two hundred and eight." " Wrong 1 You have only two hundred and seven," Ethel triumphantly: "Yes, but I swallowed a fish bone at breakfast this morning." MABEL: "So you have broken the engagement. Have you returned his ring?" " Amy : "Why, no Of course, I have changed my opinion of George, but I admire the ring just as much as ever. HE: rm surprised to see a bird on the hat of a tender-hearted woman like you. I could never bear to wear anything that cost the life of an innocent animal. She: I suppose that the calf from the hide of which your shoes are made died of old age. HE had come on her dozing in a hammock, and when she woke up she accused him of stealing a kiss. "Well." he said, " I will admit that tho temptation was too strong to be resisted. I did steal one little kiss." " One !" she exclaimed in- dignantly; "1 counted eight before I woke up." HIS EXPERIENCE.—" Ah," said the gushing spinster, " bnt you have never known what it is to have loved and lost " "I haven't, eh?" re- plied the male listener. " Well, I came pretty near it the time my wife's father dropped every penny he had on a bogus gold mine just three days before our wedding." NOT SO STRANGE. -Bighead: "Isn't it strange that men who make money in one place always go to another place to spend it?" Cynic: "Not at all. By going to another place they don't have to live down the reputation they acquired while snaking their money. E LEIS= WAY.—Mrs. Gray: "You say Mrs. Green disagreed with everything I said? Just like her! She never is on the right side of any question." Mrs. Brown "You misunderstood me. I said she agreed with everything you said." Mrs. Gray: " /I'm ! That's a way she has of currying favour." MORE LITERAL THAN MEANT.—Bobby labori- ously lugged a pail into the parlour, wnere the family was assembled, and asked the maternal grandmother to kick it. "Why should I kick it, Bobby?" grandma inquired in amazement. "Just to amuse pa," said Bobby. "He said he woula give £5 any time to see you kick the bucket !" THE AWFUL SMALL BOY.—Mrs. Cakebread Was entertaining some ladies at a select little 5 o'clock tea, and Bobby, who had been exeeptionally well behaved, was in high feather. " Ma, 'he said, as cake was being handed round, "may I have some tongue, please?" "There isn't any tongue, Bobby." "That's funny," commented Bobby, "I heard pa say there would be lots of it." FOR THE DINING-ROOM—Mrs. Fatpuree : "You paint pictures to order, don't you ? " Great Artist: Yee, madam." Mrs. Fatpurse " Well, I want a landscape with lots of deer and ducks, and quail, and partridges and pheasants, and cattle and sheep, and pigs, and so on, you know; and put a lake and an ocean in fresh and salt water, you . know; and be sure to have plenty of fish swim- nine about, because it's for the dining-room." pLEs

LADIES' LETTER:

MY DLLs NELLIE.

There are a number of charming hats being worn this winter. One which is of black silk has a band of ermine across the front, which brings it into harmony with the costume. This was worn with a tight-fitting cloth coat, which may match the skirt or be in black, dark grey, or dark blue cloth. A sort of dull bottle-green is in favour, and nearly every shade of red is worn in coats this season. This shape, with tho basque added on, is more easily fitted to the figure than those in which the cloth is in one piece from neck to hem. The double-breasted shape makes it very protective, and the way in which the fur is put on is much safer than that which carries it up into the throat. Another hat has patterns of string•coloured lace laid round the velvet brim, and a tuft of dull gold leaves resting on the hair at the lef side.

ffor out ,uroy, which is a species of velveteen, is very much worn this winter, especially in beaver brown and mouse colours. The pale mouse tint looks very well in this, and also in stamped velvet. The silver-fox fur, that has a rich golden tan colour arthe centre, is much used in trimming these indefinite and refined shades. A mouse-grey corduroy, is made without any trimming whatever, ifnd is worn with a white silk ruffle and muff, both abundantly trimmed with this delicate-looking, pretty fur. Another corduroy is in a shade of forest brown, with a hint of green in it, and has a bolero coat of brown velvet, lined with orange satin, and bordered with an inch-wide line of beaver. Chinchilla and caracul are, however, the favourite furs next to sable, which everyone would prefer to all others, but which only the well-to-do can afford. Mink is in enormous demand as the best substitute for sable. It is much cheaper. On a walking cos- tume the edges of the flounces are finished with rows of stitching. The bodice is slightly pointed in front, and has short habit tails at the back, in the new style. The fronts are covered with Paisley-patterned panne, and the tight cuffs match it. The neat black satin tie has a little lawn collar turned down over it. The muff and boa are mink, and the high-crowned hat is black velvet, with ostrich feathers and, under the brim, a knot of panne.

A very fashionable kind of Nee has the filet ground, that is, the mesh 19 large, and open. It is decidedly decorative, and when in use for blouses or dress looks particularly well over a foundation of tissu d'or or cloth of silver. Sometimes the mesh of the lace itself is formed of find gold or silver thread, whilst the pattern is in the ordinary lace thread, the combination being an original and effective one. Cluny and point cl'arabe are the laces most in vogue for trilamingr and appliques on blouses and gowns for day wear, the latter being much associated with the pretty new flannel blouses, and with or without fur on the collars of outdoor cloaks and jackets. An effective trimming for tailor gowns, or as revers and collars, is white breitschwanz, embroidered round the edges with an applique design in black coloured velvet, outlined with fine silk cord. A dark blue cloth coat and skirt had a deep collar of this kind on the coat, the applique being in navy blue velvet; a narrow scalloped band of the same ran round the skirt just below the knees, heading the shaped volant of navy blue velvet. This white breitschwanz is a charming material of a rich cream velvety appearance, and deserves to be more utilised in dress and millinery than it is.

For afternoon wear a grey cloth trimmed with. chinchilla is worp, with a front of grey satin, veiled with grey chiffon trimmed with lines of fur, and sewn thickly with silver sequins between these lines. The front of a bright terracotta velvet dress is made of the palest prawn pink satin, with ivory chiffon pleated over it, and bands of exquisitely tinted embroidery laid rather loosely over it and slightly pouched. Collars, whether of chiffon, satin or embroidery, are worn high enough to completely cover the neck, but this is now managed without the very disagreeable and incon- venient pieces of stall that used to uphold them, and at the same time seriously annoy the neck. The long silk ties are meant to be wound three times round the neck, and this makes them very warm and protective in cold weather. Your loving JENNIE. i

THE EXAMINER FRIDAY DECEMBER 27, 101

(G'opyright.)

the transport had left Las Palmas and was on the last sblv of her lumbering journey home Mark had taken it as a matter of course that he would go direct Lo Hill Top Farm to complete his recovery there instead of being detained at a military hospital. One thing only troubled him concerning his home-going, and that was the thought of the inter- view which he must have with Briggs father. He shrank from the idea of giving details of what had happened, and tried to think that it would be enough to tell the story to the vicar of his parish, and get him to re-tell it to the father; but in the end lie made up his mind that no such escape from his task was justifiable, and that he himself must see the message through. He busied himself, too, in arranging the time and manner of his wedding, in so far, being a snare man, as it was open for him to do; and in particular tried to hit upon some suitable reception for Lizzie when she should return home. _

Her absence was the only drawback now to his complete satisfaction and happiness, and when, not much within the thirty days, the transport. entered the Thames and got into dock, he sighed to think that while he was once more safe in England, she was still more than six thousand miles away, exposed to the all the rigours and evils of a vast military undertaking. By this time the coming and going of transports was so much a question of routine that there was scarcely more commotion over the glorified tramp with her human freight than there was over any other steamer with her general goods. The dis- embarkation and disposition of sick and wounded Men had been brought to the level of a well-con- ducted business, and Mark, like the rest, was dealt with pretty much as if he had been a bale of merchandise. But this did not trouble him. He recognised that in war a unit was a unit only, and that the best way of dealing with it was to treat it as a part of some machinery which could easily be replaced in case a need. Few there were on the trans-port who were well enough to pass out of the hands of the doctors and •go on furlough to their homes; but amongst the few was Mark. An exananatian of his leg was made, and he was ordered to hold himself in readiness for an operation at Netley a month after the date on which he was informed that he could make the best of his way home. "Operation!" The word rankled in Mark's mind as he sought a quiet smoking compartment at King's Cross. He disliked it, and feeling' some- what as he had felt in the old days before he became a real soldier, he determined that if he could evade the 'undertaking he would certainly do so. Be had tried to get some inkling of the sort of operation that would be needful, and for his pains' had been curtly, told to mind his own business.

"There was one thing about the saw-bones," thought Mark, "he treated me at any rate as if I was a soldier. He couldn't have been more uncivil if he'd been a gaoler talking to a prisoner. Dis- cipline's discipline, and the army's the army; but, all the same, for two pins I'd have given him a piece of my mind. Confound the man I Go back to Netley—not if I know it! Itt a spirit of rebellion and disgust he made him- self comfortable in a corner of the compartment, and was glad when the train steamed out of the station and he found that he had the luck to be alone.

Before leaving King's Cross Mark, after much reflection, had spent sixpence on a telegram to his father. He inerelY said in it: "Corning home to- day. All right.—Mitnx." "It's a waste of money'," he growled, as he hobbled to his seat. "I might just as well leave it and walk in as usual. After all, it's beginning to seem as if I'd never been away. Besides, what is there to ,make a fuss about?"

Mark remained very phlegmatic Until he got to Grantham; then he had some mild excitement in capturing by the arm a refreshment boy who was wheeling a travelling buffet on the platform. From this juvenile he demanded tea and bread and butter, and he was supplied in feverish haste. In his excitement the youth forgot, to secure his six- pence before releasing his bold of the cup. Instead be requiredlo know, in one breath, whether Marl was back from the war, whether he had seen Kruger, how many Boers he had killed, and whether he had shot or bayoneted them. Mark, with a smile, said briefly that he had been to the war, that be had beheld the President, and that he had not killed any Boers; at any rate, he hoped not. "Why," said the disgusted bay, "you're no sort of a soldier, you aren't. There was a chap come through 'ere yesterday, a 'lghlander, who told me 'e'd curled old Kroojer's whiskerl an' piled up a lot o' Boers with 'is baynit—made a regl'ar lopje of '

"Did'he really? "- asked .Mark, sarcastically. ' "He did," answered the youth. ."But, you see, he was a real soldier. You're a Yeomanry, aren't you?" The trooper laughed aloud. "Ye, you've hit it," he said; handing back his cup. "I'm only a Yeoman. I say, Johnnie, hadn't you better fasten your tea-pot down ? " "What for ? "inquired the youth in astonishment. I "Because it's so strong it'll be walking off with your cart," said Mark. -He laughed aloud again, and louder when he saw the boy's amazed look. I But the juvenile got the last word in as the train moved off. Leaving his waggon and stepping on to the footboard he pushed his head into Mark's compartment and said: "Anyway, it's a dashed sight better nor you got at the war. They didn't give you such a fat lot, of grub. Yah I " I He dropped off, as much like a porter as he could, and wheeled his vehicle on to the up plat- form, in readiness to meet the calls of other travellers. 1 .

...7.611;-lihle adventure put the crowning touch to Mark's high spirits. He walked about the compart- ment, laughed and sang to himself, and looked lovingly and delightedly at the fields and hedges, churches, houses, villages and towns through which he passed. I There was only one change for him to make, and as he found a train waiting at the station IA was home just as darkness set in. Strangely enough. as he crunched the gravel in front of the house, he felt almost an aversion to entering; but only for an instant. The joy of his return was too great to allow any such feeling to remain, and with all the air of health and fitness he could muster he opened the door of the old familiar living-room. His father, mother and sister were standing there, their forms looming large againit the -light of the lamp on the table. There was a dead silence for some seconds, then Mark said in a quiet voice: "Hullo, all. I'm back, you see, and not a pin the worse." The old farmer came up to him and put a hand on his son's shoulder. With the other he gripped Mark's hand long and hard. Still without uttering a word he drew his own chair up to the table and gently thrust the trooper into it. When he did find his tongue he said to his wife and daughter: "Well, it's one way of welcoming a lad back to stare at him like stuck sheep. Come, let's have tea." Whether he addressed his rife or his daughter was not clear, but it was immaterial. Neither took any notice him. Simultaneously they fell upon and wept Art fully over Mark. "Come, come," he said, removing their hands; "that'll do." "Aye, aye," said the farmer. "Don't make him look soft." "We couldn't look softer than you do," said the daughter with affectionate acerbity; and as it was considered that a lavish enough display of feeling had been made the four becanie very sober all at once, and gathered in quiet happiness about the table.

WOMEN'S CHAT.

The Queen, when Princess of Wales, was never so happy as when doing her Christmas shopping, going herself to the different establishments, accompanied by one or two of her daughteis• Things however are now altered. The Princess of Wales could go sboppicg incognito, but the Queen can hardly do so with the strict privacy that was formerly possible. Consequently the selection of Christmas and New Year gifts had to be made at Marlborough House, a vast assortment of suitable articles being sent there by the leading estab- lishments for this purpose. Her Majesty makes innumer- able presents at Christmas, not ouly to those near and dear to ber,but also to the members of the various Royal Households. Amongst these gifts, silver and silver- mounted articles usually figure very largely as they offer an infinite variety of choice for the dressing-table, the work-basket, the dining-room,the writing-table,and for personal ornaments. A 1 the world knows both the King and Queen Con sort are devoted to dogs, beside other animal pets, and there are acme that always eccomrany the Court, wherever it goes.even for the briefest stay. On the occa- sion ef their Majesties' recent visit to London, to com- plete the Christmas shopping, no fewer than five or six dogs, and three or four bird-cages, travelled with the Court. Needless to say both the King and Queen study the comfort of their favorites in every possible way,and at Sandringham, and other Royal residences, there is always.a dog's hoepitall and. nursery: provided for. . .

Until quite recent times, the hato-6; restaurants, anl seaside boarding houses, were deserted ai Christmas time, but now, it is no uncommon thing for them to be as crowded as at their busiest season of the year. One reason is that the custcmary family party, which as everyone kaows, sometimes leads to jars and disagree- ments, is, to some xtent going out of favour. Another is the: tendency to take advantage of the few days off to secure a change, and it is fast becoming the accepted creed that a holiday is best spent away from one's family and usual surroundings. Contequently many who are not controlled by family ties, are now found at this season of the year at the public places of enter- tainment.

When the Prince and Princess of Wales were ,ecently entertained at the Guildhall, the Humanitarian League protested against the provision of larks, and pate de foie gras, at the banquet. Certainly there seems no need in these days for the destruction of song birds in order to provide a table delicacy, and the attempt to make the thrush a bonne bouche, is also wholy to be condemned. In former times thrushes were fattened for this purpose and on a few occasions they have recently reappeared on the menus at fashionable dinner parties. As for tate de foie gras, this is provided by a species of cruelty, the goose being confined within a box without power to move, and " crammed " with spiced food until the bird's liver Ott( ills to an enormous ace. If however we knew all that went on to provide the luxuries ot the dinner table, there would perhaps b 3 many more cf us nclined to become VPflt tirians.

_ . Vegetarians are said to be increasing in number, and they may certainly claim to be making half-way con- verts of the many people wha go in for fruit breakfasts - This Custom has row a large vogue amongst !Imlay women, roany.of whom practice severe self-denial with regard to diet, for tho sake of their appearance. They eschew tea, sugar, wine, and all dainties, besides going in for fatiguing exercise, and live strictly upon a regi- men consisting very largely of vegetables. The eating of nothing but fruit before mid-day is, however, more wide'y adopted for the sake of health, and there is no room for two opinions about its wholesomeness, or ben( - ficial effect, especially to those who at other times are large meat eaters.

The " ready made " departments are, without doubt one of the most remarkable developments of modern shopping. A very little while since, well-dressed women would never have dreamed of patronising them, but tprough the exercise of steer good ta ite, and by offering ric ally worthy models, reale of the most beautiful materials, leading shops have induced quite modish individuals to alter their opinions on tt e subject. Ready-made blouses, coats and ekirts, evening gowns, all are possible nowadays, moreover, no tremor of alarm need assail the fashionab'e woman, who requires a toilette of any description in a hurry—for models are made in many sizes, and it is nearly always possible, at the cost of a little patience, to find one t 3 fit at once, and perfectly. Many society mothers, who have two or three daughters " out," are this winter dressing them alike. This has long been the custom, of course, where twins are concerned, but in any cane the effect is distinctly good. Many a pair,or trio of sisters, though fairly good looking, and well set up, would be quite unnoticeatle, ifigowned differently,but dressed aliket tt ey accentuate each other, and the simplest toilet becomes striking. The idea has a certain amount of extra enema attached to it, but the effect well repays for all this. Though lace dresses are rather waning in fashion, the lace coat, and the lace bolero continue to play their part in dress, especially with tea gowns, the former with the full belted variety, of some soft clinging fabric, such as ciepe de chine, or chiffon, over which the lace coat bar gs loose, ano the latter with Empire gowns. These, however, are comparatively little worn, despite all attempts on the put of French modistes th " boom " them again. We cherish our waist line, evdn after it has emerged,froto anything bordering on stylish- like, and loose and flowing draperies always savor to us of either mglii c or fancy dress in neither of which the generality of us tel comfortable, or lock at home. The little Russian cat), with or without the small baEques, and the habit tails at the back,are much preferred by up-"o-date dressers to the three-quarter variety. Certainly they have a much smarter appearance, and one can always don a good thick underskirt when extra warmth is needed about tte hips. One reason why the three- quarter coat is to a great ex'ent avoided is the difficulty a wearer experiences in raising s lengthy skirt. More- over, she looks, when endeavouring to do this, exactly as if she had had a very bad twist and would never come straight again. With the short skirt the three- quarter coat is never a succese,h wcver tail and graceful the wearer may be. SomFose once said that the only drawback to cur towns and cities was tl at they were sorrounded by cr uttry. Few will go so far as that in tin ir dislike of rural solitude, but there are a great many people who pref-r any kind of exister ce in the towns, to the dreary dullness of village life. Unfortunattly no one has yet discovered ready means of making country life more attractive to working people. The average villager cannot conceive that his wife) nerds any otner outlook tba n she obtains from her cottage door. raor any other rcc,estion than gossiping with her neighbours.—while he himself discusses things in general at the publichouse. But anyone who will assist in promoting social and intellectual entertainments may do something to com- bat this state of things, and it is at this time of the year that such efforts are most needed to make life more tolerable and attractive in our country towns and villages. CIrAPTER XXIIL

TROOPING HOMEWARD. Errors, large and small, were not so infrequent in the course of the war that such a mistake as the going of Benn towards the transport instead of the operating theatre should attract attention. As a matter of fact it'did not. , At the hospital itself no one seemed to know or care about the occurrence except Donald and Lizzie, and discovering this to be the ease, the surgeon promptly determined that so far as he was con- cerned the matter was ended. The subject, too, had passed from the memories of the orderlies, and if he sergeant gave it a thought at all: that thought was merely a passing one, and was not, in his own interests, encouraged. It was enough for him that the nurse had undertaken to be responsible for all that might go wrong. For the rest of the day Donald worried himself as to what he should do to cover the mistake, clear himself, if need be, and make things smooth • for Lizzie. He was more than ever admiring her ingenuity and ,resource, and quite ready to sink his own anger in his satisfaction at her success, for now he was beginning really to be glad that he was so easily rid of a troublesome business. "We're not infallible," he said to himself in con- fidence, "and it may be that the man's iron con- stitution will do more for him than my instruments. Anyway, now that he's off our hands and on some- one else's I've no further concern with him. The principal persoirof interest is the nurse. I must keep a sharp eye upon her. We cae't do with this smuggling business as a general thing." Donald did keep an eye on Lizzie, with the result that in less than a week he was satisfied that unless she also got clear of the hospital she would be a bigger trouble than Mark had been. Incessant labour and anxiety had told on Lizzie, and now that the excitement concerning Mark had lessened she was collapsing. The surgeon told her so. She bravely tried to refute his assertion, and to claiin that she was still as well fit for duty as any woman at the station. "But I say no, exclaimed the surgeon, "you're not fit. You're fagged out, you need a rest, and you will have to go away in spite of. all you've said. It would be a very serious thing to have nurses thrown on our hands here. You must go before you're bowled over, and that wilt be before long, too, if you're not careful. I'll see to it. Suppose you start to-morrow ?" "If you insist," said Lizzie, "I shall have to." "I do insist," replied Donnld. "I suppose you'd prefer to go to the same Tlace as the other patients?" He asked the question with an amiable smile. "'Well," said Lizzie, frankly, "if I'm to go at all, yes."' "You can be so useful, too, can't you ?" cona tinned Donald, growing in sarcasm and good humour. "But, mind you, no more performances like the last. That's the sort of thing you can only do once successfully." , "But it isn't necessary' to do it twice," said Lizzie. "Mark will be prefly well all right before he gets home if—if the doctors will let him." Donald laughed. "Come," lie said,"that's a pretty hard rap ; but it's a woman's privilege to use her tongue. Yours is as sharp as a scalpel, and a lot more dangerous. Well, I'm 'going to say good-bye to you now and get it over. We've seen a lot together, and I shall often think about you. Perhaps some day I may be lucky enough to look you up, if this conitry deesn't grip me too hard. It's got 'hundreds of better men in its clutches-tso hard that it will never let them go. I suppose it's the price of conquest." Donald sighed. He was much softer-hearted than he would ever admit or confess, and the loss of life in the campaign had filled him with grief. • "Yes," he added, 'the butcher's bill is heavf. I'm not politician enough to say whether it will prove to have been worth the liquidation. Well, good-bye, and good luck. Let me know when it comes off. Favour me by not doing one thing-.- . don't let him get married in khaki. I shall hate the colour to my dying pay." "It would remind me too much of things I shall want to forget," answered Lizzie, taking as a matter of course Donald's reference to the wedding. "Good-bye to you, too, doctor." She was not an emotional nurse, but there was something Very Much like a catch in her voice and something suspiciously like tears in her eyes as she spoke ard held out her hand. S "Come, you've no need to be in the dumps," said Donald, with a brave show of cheerfulness. "The luck's all on your side. Now I'm really off." He shook hands and walked quickly away. "Think God ! " he muttered as she left. "There's some good things can come even out of wars like \ this. It's made a woman of that girl, and made a man of that Yeoman chap. For anything I know it may, before it's finished, make physicians and surgeons of some of us." Lizzie went away early, next morning, worn out and restless. She was, in truth, over-worked and over-wrought, and even if Donald had not used his good offices to get her removed she must have become incapacitated very soon. When she reached Capetown she was amazed to find that Mark had already sailed for England. If there had been any slowness on the part of transport bodies they were making atonement for it now. Ship after ship was sailing with her load of sick and wounded, so full and so fast that it seemed as if there were some feverish anxiety to clear the country of anyone who could by any possibility be looked upon as a burden. - Apparently it had been assumed that Mark was either well enough to go home or bad enough to enter hospital in his own country. Whatever the cause was, the result was the same—he was actually on his way back to England; and bitter as .her disappointment was at being separated from him in that way, Lizzie was profoundly glad and thank- ful that it was his fortune to be on the sea and not an inmate of the base hospital still. She had missed him, but was just in time to see the smoke of the departing steamer on which sailed, and in time to hear the comments of those who watched. "That old tub'll be a month in doin' the trip," said a soldier near her. "I came out in her an' we were thirty days, an' swinish at that; but, by Jove I wouldn't I just be willin' to do my time in the stokin'-place if I could only go back 'ome with her !" / Lizzie turned aside. There was so much yearn- ing in the soldier's tones that she felt almost ashamed of her joy at her good fortune—almnst ashamed of having Mark blear of the country, and 'of herself for being so near to following him. Mark, too, was enduring mixed sensations. He was glad to get away, thankful to be on the sea, homeward bound, even though he lay on his back in bed; but now that he was going he had almost a fierce regret at parting from a country which had opened out such an amazing life for him, so swiftly turned him from a country ydkel to a man of action and resource; and there was the real grief of knowing that Lizzie was left behind. Before he had been a full day at sea he was so unhappy because of his position that there was a strong prospect of a serious relapse fnr him. M 3 saw this, remembered Lizzie's parting injunction, and made a brave effort to reconcile himself to his lot, and hope that all would be well very soon. Day after day the slow, ponderous ocean tramp, promoted by the exigencies Of war and the dearth of better Shipping to rank as a transport, shoved through the seas ; and day after day Mirk improved in a way which astonished all about him. It was not so much progressing as leaping towards recovery. Within a fortnight Mark was out of bed, before another week had passed he was abr.° to climb on deck and sit there, and in a day or two more he could hobble about and lean over the rail, easing his wounded leg by supporting himeelf on the other. At the end of the third week he was almost tempted to declare that he was a com- pletely cured man, and to challenge anyone approximating his own condition to a,walk or race across or up and down the deck. If there was any merriment he joined in it; arid none laughed louder than he when a convalescent, who was coming home with an arm and a leg short, declared that the ship had lost her way and would never land in England at all. Like most of the rest Mark was seen forgetting the sufferings and perils through which he had passed. His escape from Pretoria became so shadowy at titnes in his remembrance that lie was near forgetting it, and his privations on the veld also began to Appear less serious as the days passed. I Even his wound, improving as it was, troubled him hardly more than one or two severe accidents he had met with in his farming life, and by the timo

..... "Fighting's made you quite selfish, I see," said his sister. "Here, take my arm, and let me give, you a lift." whey walked slowly to Jackson's farm, and unceremoniously entered. No sooner had Jackson seen who stood before him than he smote the arm of his chair, exclaiming: "Well, I niyver—this beats all !" and fell into what ',seemed an endless chuckle. _ _ _ _

Mark was stupefied. He did not.speak, nor did he so much as offer his hand. "Come," said Jackson's sister--an austere old woman in a terrific black bonnet and mittens— "Come ; enough of this tomfoolery! Sit in , this chair, Mark. He's been like this these last three days. I (19n't know what he's thinkin' oxi. It seems to me there's not but bother wi' young fowk. I remember when my gran'mother---" "Oh, that'll do, that'll do, broke in Jackson. "We want no more about old women. We've heard enough of 'em. Give young uns a turn, eh, lad?"

He turned with an extraordinarily contorted face to Mark, and chuckled again. Mark looked helplessly about him—looked slowly round the room. But no one came to his assistance. Even his faithful sister did not appear te be at all willing to shed light , upon this extra- o dinary behaviour of the old farmer: a man whom Mark had expected to find in the depths of misery. So far from being depressed, however, he was actually in a state of the most unseemly mirth. It may have been fancy on (lie trooper's part; but as his glance travelled round the room, he thought he saw a movement of the parlour door, as if someone: had been on the po nt of coming out of the room I and had stopped short on seeing him. "I was told you wanted to ask me about Lizzie," said Mark, who began nowito wish.that he had , refused to come. "Lizzie! Oh, aye. Ho, ho!" said Jackson. ' "So I do, so I do. I want to know about Lizzie very partic'lar. I just want to clap my eyes on her, then I shall be happy! Only that." "Then I'm afraid you'll have to wait a bit," said Mark. .

"Shall I? Oh," said the farmer, "we,shall sea about that. How is Lizzie, my lad ? " "When I last saw her she was looking very poorly," said Mark, incensed at what he took to be the callousness of the old farmer.

"An' when I saw 'er last she was looking remarkably well. Ho, ho I " said Jackson. He had another fit of chuckling, and his sister, with savage hits upon his back, urged him not to be such an old fool. § "But I can't 'elp it—l really can't," piped the farmer. "Mark were a bit surprised when he went away; but it's nothing to his surprise now, eh, Mark? What 'lid you say if I was to tell you 'at 77

But it was 'not needful for him to say more. Mark stood up as straight as if he had been slio9ked into rigidity, for, standing in the parlous dom4ay was Lizzie herself! She remained there only for an instant. With a cry, she rushed across the floor, and was locked in Mark's outstretched arms.

Again Jackson tried to say "Ho, ho," in a cheerful manner, but his effort resulted only in an alarming croak. Seizing this pretext to get him into the back kitchen, on the ground that he wanted snore and cooler air, the black-bonneted lady led him from the living-room; and, on the feeble excuse of giving help, Mark's sister left the room, 10 that the lovers were entirely alone.

"Come on," said Mark, .r..ating himself boldly in the vacated chair and drawing Lizzie towards him. "Just you make yourself comfortable here. and tell me how it's all happened." "You're forgetting that you're an invalided soldier," said Lizzie, with a happy smile—she had become very hot and red all at once; but that may- be attributed to her nearness to a blazing fire. "Soldiers be bothered I" exclaimed Mark. "As for invalids—do I feel like one ?"

He put a strong arm round Lizzie's waist and drew her towards him; but she refused to under- stand his wish. .

"Well, all right," he said, "if you think it isn't safe enough there, just coins and sit alongside;. there's plenty of room in this chair for two like us." Literally, it was a very loose fit, but they were both much too happy to consider trifling disadvan- tages like that. "Now," said Mark, "jtist you make one or two things clear. There's nobody watching "--he kissed her as he spoke—"and I shouldn't care if people were. You're mine now, my girl; and I never mean to let you go. If the dad, or the aunt—bless her bonnet, where did she get it ?—or the sister come in, never mind 'era. They're only members of the family."

(To be concluded.)

KNOWLEDGE.

Knowledge is gained with pain and ;Jost with ease. Do any of us remember how we learned the alphabet? Here is one who does. "What is this ? " inquired the tescherOpointing to a huge capital A. The question was repeated, but failed toslicit • reply. "I want you to tell me the name of this letter." said the teacher again. "'You know the name of it,"was the rejoinder. " Yes, I know the name of it," admitted the teacher, " but want to find out whetoer vou know its name or not. "Oh ! don't trouble °tout rap," said the little boy "But it's my businees to trouble about you—l mu trouble about you," persisted the worried teacher " why won't you tell me the name of this letter ? " " Because if I do, Miss you'll only want to know the name of the next one."

Knowledge is power." There is no darkness but ignor- ance." said Shakespeare ; but then Shakespeare never travelled on tha Metropolitan Railway. The most valuable knowledge is that taught by Experience. Thcso unfortunate beings who are racked by the agonies of sciatica will do well to profit by the experience of Mr Croft, of Brenley Farm, Boughton, Faversham. Says Mrs Harriet Croft "In the early part of a cold February my husband had a painful illness. He was very busy just then, and once found it necessary to re- main at his post the whole night through. The weather was wet and cold—so bad that he was thoroughly chilled, and a severe cold resulted. Sciatica the doctor called it,and treated him accordingly; but his medicines failed to do any good. Be became worse, and suffered excruciating pain. Far many days be was unable to put his foot to the ground. We consulted a second doc- tor. and later a. third. One blistered him, the other painted the part affected; but nothing did him any good. In August of the same year a small book reached us, and in it we read of the cure, by Seigel's Syrup, of a Mr Day,who lived at Cowden, Edenbridge" Having written to Mr Day,and been assured by him that every word said About his case was true, Mrs Croft re- solved togive Seigel'eSyrup a trial." Alter the first bottle" continues Mn, Croft, " there was a marked improve- ment, and by the time he had finished the second, he walked three miles and did a day's work. When there's a long spell of wet, my husband nets a twinge now and then, but Selei's Syrup never fails to quickly set him right," Thousands might very profitably allow Mr Croft's ex- perience to serve in lieu of their own. That is valuable knowledge, easily acquired. In connection with our Carnegie Competition we have awarded One Guinea to the following :—Matilda Childs, Medstead, near Alresford, Hants; Edith Gourley, 17 Abbey Terrace, Gateshead; Mrs John Bradshaw, En- glesa Brook, Barthomley, near Crewe ; Ethel Weavers. Halstead-road, Twinstead, Sudbury. Suffolk; Rev G W Bennett, 17, George-btreet, Worksop, Notts.

THE Fli-IST YEAR OF, THE CENTURY.

The first year of the Century Has passed the shcrtest day, This year of many sad events Will soon have passed away. The noble Queen we loved so well Is with us now no more, We bear the war with fortitude. But wish that it were c'er. The Cettury began with war, Calamity and Etrife ; In China horrors shocked the world, And da•kened human life. We think the clouds ate breakiog now, That better days are near ; And if we trust in Providence, Whit need have we to !fear. Above the darkest stormy clouds Unseen by human eye, The wondrous orbs of light shine on Through al the azure sky. The old year soon will pass away And winter winds will blow, When it has gone, forever goae, As all on earth must go. How many friends alas we mourn, Since this year's dawn began, Where shall we find unsullied peace And happiness for man. When this uncertain life i 3 o'er Our only hope must be, A better wo,ld beyond the grave Which now we cannot see.

S. KENYON. Warrington, Decomber, 1901.

AN AGED TREE.

Thi4 lonely tree. how Sere it lie, O'ercome by time at last ; But, 0, what Visions round it rise From out the dreamy past ! When lovely Spring's reviving powers Had cleared the snows away, And waked the birds and early flowers, To bless the lengthlning day : 0, then bedecked in tender green, It stood erect and high ; A glory to the rural scene That charmed the passers-by. The wild birds loved its leafy shade, And sang their anthems there ; While village children round it played With joy that knew no care. And in the Summer's sultry noon, The wayfarer opprest, Would hail its shadow as a boQn, And sit him down to rest. Then in the russet Autumn e'en, When slow the sun went down, 'Twas joy to watch his golden sheen Gleam through its leafy crown. Nor less, to see its noble form, When Winter swept the plain, All graceful bow before the storm. And graceful rise again. 0 happy they who, like that tree, Their seasons so falai. That in their wal s the world may sea New charms, now blessings still. When they, like it, all full of days, No more life's course pursue Men will recall their kindly ways, And bless their memory too. J. H. W. CLINDINNING.

1901 At Home and Abroad.

QUEEN VICTORIA AND KING EDWARD. Incomparably the greatest event in the world's history during the first year of the new century has been that which occurred on the 22nd January, when Queen Victoria passed away at Osborne, and the crown she had worn for 63 years passed to her son, King Edward VII. For many months the Queen's health had been steadily failing, though knowledge of the fact was studiously withheld from the public, and it was not till the evening of Jan. 18th that any official intimation was made of her illness. But the wording of the bulletins of that day and the next quickly prepared the minds of her subjects for the worst. The solemn quiet of awed expectancy with which the news of the arrival of the supreme moment was awaited throughout the length and breadth of her great Empire, and wherever Englishmen were gathered in foreign lands, will never be for- gotten by those who saw it. It was surpassed only by the universal expressions of love for the dead and sympathy for those she had left behind, when at last the news was flashed to the ends of the earth that the great Queen had breathed her last "surrounded by her children and grandchildren." But distraction from the grief which the close of one reign created was provided by the instant necessity of in- augurating the new reign which followed. The Prince of Wales proceeded to London early on the morning following his mother's death, and at a Privy Council meeting in St. James's Palace he was declared King under the title of Edward VII., and received the oath of allegiance of the principal officers of State. In brief and dignified but intensely earnest speech the new King declared that his constant endeavour would always be to walk in the foot- steps of his beloved mother, and that be was "fully determined to be a Constitutional

The Late Queen. The Late Empre The Late The Late President McKinley. Ameer of Afghanistan.

Sovereign in the strictest sense of the word, and as long as there was breath in his body to work for the good and amelioration of his people." On Feb. Ist the remains of the late Queen were conveyed from Cowes to• Portsmouth between a double line of British and foreign ships of war. On the 2nd they were carried across London in the presence of a vast concourse of her mourn- ing subjects, followed by the new King, the mporor of Germany, the King of the Belgians, 1.-0 King of Portugal, the King of Greece, the Queen, and numerous Princesses in carriages, and a brilliant procession of Princes and soldiers. On arrival at Windsor a funeral

service was held in St. George's Chapel, and simultaneously, in St. Paul's, in Westminster Abbey, and in idnumerable churches and chapels throughout the country, the day being universally set apart as one of national mourn- ing. Finally, on Feb. 4th, the Royal remains - were hid to rest in the Mausoleum at Frogmore side by side with those of the late Prince Consort. Next clay King Edward issued a, -series of proclamations expressive of his grati- tude for and appreciation of the sympathy of 'Lis new subjects at home and abroad in his great grief. The King opened the first working session of his first Parliamentin person on Feb. 14th, accompanied by Queen Alexandra. The ceremony, though only conducted in "semi- State," was an exceedingly brilliant and im- pressive one. Among the Acts of the ensuing , session was one enabling his Majesty to amend his title, and this he did by proclamation on -Nov. 4th. His Majesty is now "By the Grace ,of God, of the United Kingdom of Great Britain and Ireland and of the British Dominions beyond the Seas King, Defender of the Faith, Emperor of India." Another bereavement befeL cur Royal Family in the death, on Aug. Zrd, of the Empress Frederick of Germany, eldest sister of' the King. The Empress had long been suffering from a painful and incurable malady. •

THE ROYAL TOUR.

'Absolutely unique in the history of the world iS the tour undertaken by the heir to the British Throne and his consort during the year that is past. The original occasion of the tour was the inauguration of the new Australian Commonwealth, but the journey once under- taken, every part of the_Empire clamoured to be included in its scope. The Duke and Duchess of Cornwall and York set sail in the Ophir from Portsmouth on March 16th.

The Queen's Funeral: Passing the Marble Arch.

Gibraltar, Malta, Ceylon, and Singapore were visited on the .outward journey, the greatest enthusiasm being manifested here, as every- where throughout the tour, by natives and Europeans alike. The ceremony of opening the Federal Parliament took place on May 9th, at Melbourne, in a vast ball containing some 10,000 people. A round of festivities fol- lowed here and at Brisbane and Sydney. A brief stay in New Zealand, where the Maori chiefs were among the most loyal of the hosts of the Royal couple, was followed by visits to Hobart Town, Adelaide, and Perth. Mauritius was touched on the further journey to South Africa. In spite of the gloom cast by the war the receotion given to the Duke and Duchess

in Natar and Cape Colony was as earnest and , hearty as elsewhere. Crossing the Atlantio Ocean, the tourists reached Quebec in the middle of September. Thence by Montreal, Ottawa, -Winnipeg, Calgary, and, ,Vancouver, they made their memorable journey through the whole length of British North America. The return by way of Toronto and through the Maritime Provinces to Halifax and St. John's, Newfoundland, was, like the outward journey, one long triumphal progress. Both in South Africa and in Canada meetings with native chiefs formed pieturesque items in the Royal itinerary. The tour ended in Yarmouth Roads on the last day in October, having thus lasted seven months and a half. Its value as a means of cementing the affection between the Mother Country and her Colonies and of both for the reigning house is beyond calculation. On Nov. Ist a landing was made at Portsmouth, the King and Queen. with the little children of the Duke and Duchess, having gone out into the Roads to meet them. The arrival in London next day WaS the occasion of a great popular, demonstration. The King's birthday a week later saw the promotion of his son to the title of Prince of Wales and Earl of Chester. The Prince and Princess attended a luncheon at the Guildhall on Dec. sth in honour of 'their return when the Prince made an exceedingly interesting speech on his impressions of his trip.

THE SOUTH AFRICAN WAR.

The 'history of the war in South Africa during the past twelve months has been for the most part a repetition of the incidents of the last three months of 1900—perpetual _chasing& of

Prince of Wales. The Prim

fugitive commandos, successive captures of larger or smaller bodies of the enemy, varied by Boer captures of parties of our own troops, and by a host of indecisive skirmishes in which now one side and now the other appeared to get the best of things. But always there has been a slow; steady, wearing down of the enemy's resources, and, in spite of the rebel accessions to their ranks from Cape Colony, an equally steady diminution of their numbers. Perhaps the most dramatic of the reverses sus- tained by our troops was that at Brakenlaagte in October, in which Col. Benson lost his life. At the same time as they hunted the Boer commandos our troops have been engaged in a systematic clearance of the means of Subsistence from the country side, in the hope of making the roving tactics of the Boers no longer pos- sible from lack of supplies. This has been followed up latterly by the establishment of lines of blockhouses intended to prevent the passage of the enemy into districts already settled. By this means Mr. Brodrick, the Secretary for War, was able to claim in November that there had already been shut in 14,700 square miles of the Transvaal and 17,000 square miles of the Orange River Colony. In the same speech the impending despatch was announced of a large body of fresh troops to replace some of the war-worn men already there. One result of this clearing policy has been the necessity of providing for the non- combatant population which it deprived of the means of subsistence. This has been done by the establishment of a number of great con- centration camps at various points on the rail- way line in the Transvaal, the Orangc River Colony, Cape Colony, and Natal. Owing to de- fective accommodation, epidemics, and the

Assassination of President McKinley.

13 oer ignorance of the laws of sanitation, the mortality in these camps, especially among the children, has been terribly high. Miss Emily Hobhouse, who drew attention to the state of things there, has since been prevented by the military authorities from landing in South Africa, and legal proceedings have resulted. The continued presence of Boer raiders in Cape Colony has been considered to render impossible the maintenance of Parliamentary government there, and the public service has been carried on by the warrants of the Governor. Martial law was also extended on Oct. 9th throughout the whole of the Colony, including Cape Town.. In February last a meeting took place between Lord Kitchener and Commandant Germ-al Botha, at which the former sketched terms of surrender with which Botha, "seemed satisfied." When, however, these terms had be sub- mitted to and revised by Mr. Chamberlain and presented to Botha in their new form, that General declared himself unable even to submit them to his Government for consideration. Since then the Boer leaders have declared that nothing but the granting of complete indepen- dence will induce them to lay down their arms. In May Sir Alfred Milner visited this country on leave of abneuce, and was given a peerage.

MR. McKINLEY'S ASSASSINATION.

The year has been marked by yet another assassination of the Head of a State—an -assassination even more purposeless than that of the King of Italy in 1900. On Friday, Sept. 6th, Mr. William McKinley, President of the United States of America, was shot while on a visit to the Pan-American Exhibition at Buffalo. His assailant, a youth named Leon Czolgosz, who had been excited by the teach- ings of Anarchist lecturers, made his attack at a great reception, while the President was in the act of offering him his hand. The President appeared for some days, with his excellent con- stitution, to have a good chance of recovery. On the seventh day, however, there was a relapse. and death took place early on Sept.

===== 0002601_19011227_art0038.txt | included=true | included =====
rHE EXAMINER. FRIDAY. DECEMBER 27 1901.

14t1f. The struggle Nail been watched with the deepest solicitude by the whole civi- lised world, and the sympathy with tie grief of the American people and Mrs. McKinley at its fatal termination was no less widespreaa.* The President's remains were conveyed to Washing- ton on the 17th, and next day 63,000 of his countrymen passed the coffin as it lay in the Capitol. On the 19th the interment took place at his own town of Canton, Ohio. The assassin was tried and condemned on the 25th for murder, and was " electrocuted " at Auburn on Oct. 29th. On Mr. McKinley's death Colonel Theodore Roosevelt, the Vice-President, became, by the provision of the Constitution, President in his stead. He at once declared his intention of carrying on unaltered the policy of his predecessor. He is however, a known enemy of the great Trust; which dominate in- dustrial and political life in the States, and he has set his face against the custom of making official appointments the reward of party ser- vices. The defeat of Tammany Hall at the New York Mayoral elections in November may be regarded as to some degree a result of the new influence he has already brought to bear on the political life of his country. Mr. Roosevelt's first Presidential Message to Congress on Dec. 3rd recommended the exclusion and deportation of Anarchists, the adoption of legislation on trusts, and the conclusion of reciprocity treaties with foreign States.

Britain in the matter, and this treaty was brought before the Senate in December. With the capture of Aguinaldo the revolt in the Philippines has been brought to a virtual con- clusion. Civil war in Colombia has led to an American intervention in Isthmian politics, of which it is difficult to see the conclusion. The death of the Ameer, Abdurrahman Khan, in October caused some fear of complications in ,that restless kingdom. Happily, however, his son, Habibullab, was proclaimed Ameer forth- with in perfect quiet. The Federation of

-

's Last Stand.

Australia was inaugurated on Jan. Ist, and elections took place in March to the first Federal Parliament. With the aid of a small Protectionist majority in both Houses M. Barton and his colleagues are striving to carry a Protective Revenue Tariff in face of strenuous opposition. Several duties have, however, been rejected or reduced. Insufficient rains have again delayed India's recovery from the great famine. The inhabitants of Malta ,have been greatly agitated by an ordinance legalising the English language in the law courts of the island.

Dr. Lara TRADE, COMMERCE, AND

AGRICULTURE.

Tie trade of the year, though still good, slim-s increasing signs that the high-water mark cf the close of the century has been passed. Tha Board of Trade returns of imports for the first eleven months of the year show only a slight decrease in value as compared with the corresponding period in the previous year—£475,506,540, against £477,275,947—but the exports show a decrease of over eleven mi1i0n5—£256,185,112, against £267,839,334. In the textile trades Lusincs,s has remained fairly brisk, but the large profits which cotton spinners realised in 1900 have not Iteen main- tained, and weaving looms have been by no means constantly employed. The various branches of the iron trade have been fairly flourishing, though at one period in the machine-making industry particularly there was a considerable falling off of business, and

lbein

ninny men were out of work. The relations of employers and employed have been almost uniformly good. The Penrhyn quarry dispute, however, still drags on its weary way, and there was a disastrous lock-out in the fishing trade at Grimsby. Here the disputants were ultimately persuaded by Lord Yarborough to refer their differences to the arbitration of a Board of Trade nominee, but not before the offices of the Masters' Federation had been wrecked in a formidable riot. The home Stock Market has been depressed throughout the year, and consols, rails, and industrials are all lower than they were. American and Canadian rails, however, have steadily improved. African mines have maintained their prices in spite of the war, but Australians have been very erratic. An extremely dry summer has had a bad effect on agriculture. There has been a fair crop of wheat and barley, but oats have been much below the average. Hay was, of course, a very bad crop, and imports from Canada have kept down prices for what there was. Roots, too, have done poorly. Cattle and sheep have kept a fair price, and calving cows have been remarkably plentiful, but feeding prices are still going up. Cheese has sold well, and so has such butter as is still made in England. The supply of pigs is still diminishing.

destroyers, ond even more costly lesons have been learnt in military science, and a great im- petus given to invention, through the war. A most important conflict, big with consequences for the future of electric traction, has beem that between the champions of direct and polyphase current for the underground railway in London. now decided in favour of the former.. 3, The seismograph, hitherto used simply to record earthquake shocks, has been •shown to be capable of indicating atmospheric. disturbonces at a considerable dis- tance and so obtains a new value as a weather prophet. In pure scienco also much work has, been done which, however, is apt to be of less gen.?ral 'interest than that of a more practical nature. In biology and botany new species have beeq discovered, some fillingemportant gaps in the evolutionary order. Some interest- ing speculations as to the relation.s of the de— velopment of the brain and the shape of the skull have been called forth by extensive head measurements conducted mainly by Frenchmen. Lord Kelvin has suggested a theory which in— dicates that the total amount of matter in the universe may be about equal to 1,000,000,000 -times that contained in our sun. Professor G. Forbes suggests the existence of a large planet beyond Neptune. Interesting information as to the pre-Grecian and other old inhabitants' of Crete has been obtained by excavations in that island, and the Antarctic regions are being invaded by British and German expeditions acting in co-operation.

HOME POLITICS.

The first Parliamentary session of the new reign was devoted mainly to financial measures. The war charges involved the sanction of another large loan and the imposition of fresh taxation. TIN income tax was raised 2d. in the pound, a tit:ay of a halfpenny in the pound was imposed upon sugar and one of le,per ton upon exported coal. This last measure was strenuously opposed by both masters and men in the mining districts. A civil list of £470,000 was granted to the new King, several changes being introduced in the methods of its com- putation. Other financial measures included large additional schemes of expenditure upon the defensive forces of the Empire. A larae measure of Army reform was carried, the main feature of which was the creation of five Army Corps and Army Corps districts, ti) the head- quarters of which much of the routine work of the War Office was delegated. A Bill for setting up new local education authorities met with so strenuous an opposition, that it was withdrawn, and a temporary measure was passed in its stead giving the Municipal and County Councils power to authorise the continuance by the School Boards for one year of the higher grade classes ren- dered illegal by the famous Cockerton judg- ment. A comprehensive Factory Act, a Juvenile Offenders' Act, and an Act renewing for four years the Agricultural Rating and Clerical Tithes Acts were the only other measures of importance which the session produced. The most striking feature of the autumn political campaign was the reappearance of Lord Rose. bery, who, at its close, addressed a great meeting at Chesterfield. An indiscreet after- dinner speech by Sir Redvers Buller in reply to certain, criticisms of his conduct of the Natal campaign led to his dismissal from the com- mand of the First Army Corps at Aldershot. This step caused great popular agitation. A mass meeting in Hyde Park passed a resolution declaring the speech to be the excuse for and not the cause of his dismissal.

FOREIGN AND COLONIAL AFFAIRS.

The crisis in China, which formed so terrible a chapter in the history of 1900, has in theory at any rate found a satisfactory settlement in 1901. The negotiations between the pleni- potentiaries of the Empire and the represents-

Habibullah -ller. Dr. Ingram, Bishop • of London. fives of the Powers dragged on till Sept. 7th, when the final Protocol was signed in Pekin. Ten days later the Forbidden City was handed over to the Chinese, and was forthwith set in order for the return of the Court. The Court, however, has not yet arrived, though it is understood to have been on the move many weeks. The indemnity has been fixed at £65,000,000, and interest and sinking fund are secured by increased Customs 'duties. Two of the offending mandarins were executed in Pekin last February, and others have been banished by the Emperor. Just. two months after the signing of the ,Protocol Li Hung Chang, its principal Chinese negotiator, died, and with him all prospects of an early settlement with Russia in regard to her position in: Man, churirt. Serious trouble has been caused in the Near East by the refusal of Turkey to carry out her obligations under the concession made to the French Quays Com- pany at Galata. Even the departure of the French Ambassador failed to extract a satis- factory response to the French demands under this head. Nothing less than the seizure Of 71.1ytilene was required to bring_the Sultan to •hi S senses, and to obtain its release the Porte was compelled to give guarantees .for the settle- ment of many other matters of difference be- tween the two Powers besides that which caused the original rupture. Throughout the crisis France had the tacit support of Ru.s.sia, the Czar's visit to France while it was at its height giving emphasis to the fact. After a meeting with the Kaiser at Dantzig, the Czar end Czarina, reviewed the French fleet off 'Dun- kirk on Sept. 18th, and three days later they were present at a review of 130,000 French troops near Compiegne. Great excitement was created in France by the passage, in face of . strenuous opposition, of a Bill requiring all monastic orders and other associations to obtain a licence from the Government. Many religious orders have complied with the new law, but others have preferred emigration. In Belgium Bill has been carried suppressing the gaming tables at the various watering places. The hi-centenary t,f the establishment of the . Prnssinn monarchy was to have been celebrated with great elaboration last January, but the death of Queen Victoria, grandmother of the Kaiser, cut the festivities short. The Queen of Holland was married in February to Duke Henry. of Mecklenburg-Schwerin. The ex- communication of Count Tolstoi and other more • personal grievances provoked riots among the -"students of the Russian Universities in March, and these were put down with terrible brutality • and ferocity by the soldiery. The Minister of .Education was assassinalted, and the Precurator of ' the Holy Synod was, shot at. A treaty re- visine. the Anglo-American agreement respect- ing the creation of a canal from the Caribbean to the Pacific was signed in January, but was so mutilated by the United States Senate as to lead to its abandonment by Great Britain. In November, however, a fresh treaty was. sig.ned. which practically involves the abroga- tion of the whole of the rights of Great

,SCIENTIFIC PROGRESS.

A great deal of useful work has been done in 'the course of the year in various departments of science. .Among the most immediately use- ful investigations are those into the introduc- tion of arsenic intr - • nrticles of human

The metal aluminium is slowly pressing forward, and proving itself ugeful in new and important ways. Aerial navigation has made, distinct progress,- M. 'Santos Dumont having successfully steered his air ship from St. Cloud round the Eiffel Tower and back. Wireless telegraphic apparatus has been fitted to Atlantic r liners, and the Campania and Lucania have been able to communicate in mid-ocean, though quite out of sight of one another. SPORTS AND PASTIMES.

Once more British yachtsmanship has proved unequal to the task of bringing home the America Cup. The races between Sir Thomas Lipton's Shamrock 11. and the defender Columbia were very close, however, and there is every reason to hope for better luck next time. The English cricket champion- ship last summer was a foregone conclusion almost from the first, York- shire out-distancing all competitors with her wonderful all-round style. A. C. MacLaren has taken a team out to Australia which, though far from representative, has so far done remarkably well. Liverpool won theAFootball League Cup in April by splendid form from about the New Year onwards, and though their renewed success this year is improbable Everton are making a good bid to keep the trophy in the Mersey city. The English Cup was won by Tottenham Hotspur, who first made a draw before 121,000 people at the Crystal Palace, and a week later won handsomely in the re- played tie at Bolton. England did indifferently in the internationals with Scotland, drawing the Association game and losing that under Rugby rules. Devon beat Durham in , the Rugby County Championship. The popularity of hockey and its Canadian-born cousin, lacrosse, has increased bv great strides, and golf is rapiQy ceasing to be the monopoly of the well-to-do. Cycling has grown more popular than ever as a pastime and less so as a sport. 'A. A. Chase is the hero of the year for speed, and his forty miles in an hour was a remarkable performance even on a beautiful track, with motor-pacing. Auto-mobilism is increasing in popularity by leaps and bounds, and vast sums have been spent during the year on experiments.' A few running, hammer-throwing, weight-putting, and other athletic records have been made, but speaking generally the year has not been marked by that madness for record-breaking which was at one time dominant in the world of sports. Several American athletes have paid us a visit. A plucky attempt to cross the Channel was made in August by Montagu Holbein, but the task had to be given up six miles short of the English coast. Corporal Ommundsen was the winner of the King's Prize at Bisley. Indoors there has been a most remark- able growth in the demand for billiard equip- ment, full size and miniature, while " ping- Pon " has been taken up with furious energy by the young of both sexes.

OBITUARY. Death has been very busy in high places during the year that has gone. Among heads of States and members of ruling houses the deaths of our own beloved Queen and her daughter, the Empress Frederick, President McKinley, and the Ameer Abdurrahman Khan, have already been referred to and to these .1-oust be added the es-King Milan of Servia.. Statesmen and politicians who have gone in- clude Signor Crispi, the great Italian; Prince Hohenlohe. ex-Chancellor of the German Empire; Count Hatzfeldt, German Ambassa- dor in London; Li Hung Chang, the China- man; Sir J. 11. Dickson, ex-Premier of Queens- land and Minister of Public Defence in the first Federal Government; Sir E. W. Stafford, three times Premier of New Zealand; Sir John Mackenzie; Ex-Governor Eyre, of Jamaica; Mr. W. W. B. Beach, "Father" of the House of Commons; and the erratic Irish M.P. Dr. Tanner. A great permanent official has been lost to us in Sir Courtenay Boyle, Secretary to the Board of Trade. The Church

has lost Dr. Creighton, Bishop of London; Dr. Stubbs, Bishop of Oxford; Dr. Westcott, Bishop of Durham; and the Rev. H. R. Harreis. The legal profession is the poorer bY the deaths of Sir A. L. Smith, Master of the Rolls; Lord Morris; Sir Franklin Lushington, Chief Magistrate at Bow-st., and Mr. Samuel Pope, K.C., leader of the Parliamentary Bar. The Army has lost General Sir A. L. Fremantle; and the Navy Admiral of the Fleet Sir John Commerell,i of Crimea fame, and Admiral Sir Anthony Hoskins. Among departed representatives of literature are Sir Walter Besant, Mr. Robert Buchanan, Mr. W. J. Stillman, Mr. Ignativa Donnelly (of the " Cryptogram"), and Miss C. M. Yonge. In the world of music, Sir John Stainer, Signor Verdi, and Signor Piatti have left us, and in their company may be named Mr. R. D'Oyly Carte, the great operatic im- pressario. Prominent medical men are lost to us in Sir James Piget, Sir Andrew Fairbairn, and Sir W. MacOtrmac. Two explorers have died in Prince Henry of Orleans and Professor Nordenskiold. The year's obituary also in- cludes Miss Kate Greenaway, the charming painter of child life; Mr. J. M. Brydon, archi- tect of the new War Office; Miss Orrnerod, the entomologist; Lord Wantage; Sir Edward Watkin, the 'railway king ; Mr.. George Lohmann, the cricketer; Mrs. Kruger, wife of the Transvaal ex-President; the Duchess of Cleveland, mother of Lord Rosebery; and the Dowager Lady Carew, aged 103, said to have

dnnceSnt the famous uan at Brussels on the eve of Waterloo.

MISCELLANEOUS ITEMS.

A picturesque event of the year was the trial of Earl Russell by his peers, with the Lord Chancellor as Lord High Steward at their bead, for a bigamous marriage contracted in the United States. The divorce which his lordship had obtained ineAmerica from his first wife was found of no effect in British law, and he was sentenced' to three months' imprisonment as a first-class misdemeanant. Mr. Andrew Car- negie announced in June a gift of £2,000,000 for the endowment of chairs in the Scottish trniversities and the payment of the fees of ; '

Ear]

Trial in the

ouse of

poor students there. Dr. Winnington Ingram has succeeded the late Dr. Creighton as Bishop of London. The thousandth anniversary of the death of King Alfred was celebrated in September, when a statue of him was un- veiled by Lord Rosebery at Winchester, his ancient capital. The decision of the House of Lords that trades unions may be sued in the law courts to the full amount of their funds for damage caused by the action of their members created great consternation among the friends of the trades union movement. Disasters on a large scale have been numerous during the year. On Sept. 19th the torpedo destroyer Cobra, while on a trial trip with new turbine engines, broke in two in a gale, and 07 of her crew were drowned. The New York liner City of Rio de Janeiro sank in February at the mouth of San Francisco harbour, and 120 of those on board were drowned. The steamer Islander, carrying returning gold diggers, struck an ice- berg off the Alaskan coast, and over 100 of the passengers and crew were lost. Some 200 lives were lost on the British coasts through the severe storm of November, and another great gale in December caused much further havoc. Between 70 and 80 men lost their lives through an explosion in the Universal Colliery, in South Wales. Damage to the amount of some 4,000,000 dollars was done in January by a great fire in the business portion of Montreal. Another great fire at Jacksonville, Florida, rendered from 10,000 to 15,000 persons homeless. A dam burst in the State of Virginia in June, by which 500 persons were drowned.

GRAPPEN HALL.

THE DAY Sorrooz.--The annual distribution of prizes took place in the rchool last week. The awards were I given by the Rev R Greenall, over fifty scholars being recipients. The following boys and girls attended for twelve months without a single absence : Janet Fair- hurst, Emily Wheeler, John Maiowaring, Donald Mac- kenzie, Ada Wheeler, Dorothy Eaton and Cleric Eaton After the distribution the school broke up for the Christmas holidays. THE EVENING Scaom—The social in connection with the above passed off last Friday evening. About thirty pupils and friends sat down to tea. Music, dancing and games formed the programme for the rest of the night, a very enjoyable time following.

Professor Dixon in South Africa. Professor Dixon, who is well known as an enter- tainer in Liverpool and Manchester, and who has bppeared several times with marked success at the Parr Hall. Warrington, is at present doing a good work for the benefit of our " Tommies " in Kimberley, South Africa. On November 26th, the first of a series of concerts took place, the proceeds being devotel to providing a Christmas tnat for the sick and wounded soldiers in No 11 General Hospital. The concert, which was given in the Kimberley Town flail, was not so extensively patronised as it deserved to be but we are sure that Professor Dixon's kind thoughtfulness for our dis- abled soldiers in the festive season was highly appreciated in official circles. Much 'sympathy is expressed for the Professor and Mrs Dixon, owing to the recent fire at their residence. Although insured, the indirect loss in music and clothes was very heavy. Describing the fire, one of the South African newsplpers. says :—" It was a question ot saving one from ones friends, as the furniture was bundled out so quickly that it was all more or less damaged.

The Vicar-Designate of St. Chad's, Over. The Rev W D. Stables, M.A., who has accepted,at the gift of the Bishop of Chester, the living of St: Chad's Over, has earned a wide reputation as an energetic worker and organizer. He has been assis- tant curate in the :parish of Runcorn since 1893. Shortly after his advent in the parish he was ap- Tointed curate-in.charge of Weston, where he found the day school had to serve the double pur pose of educating :the children and providing a place for public worship. Together with the Vicar (the R3V Canon Maitland Wood), Mr Stables set to work to raise funds to build a church, and the movement, though at first beset with numerous difficulties, at .length took prac- tical shape by the gift of a Site and £5OO from Major Orred. The first sod was cut in February, 1897, and the foundation stone laid on May Nth of the same year by the Rev John Daniel Darby, D.D., dean of Chester. The choristers who responded to the appeal of Mr Stables may be reckoned by thousands, In commemoration of the splendid work of the boys in helping to raise funds to build the church Mr Joseph Spencer, of Ryton, Durham made a gilt to the church of a magnificent organ. At the time the foundation stone upwards of £l,OOO was still required to cover the total cost, but when the building was opened this debt had practically teen met, thanks to the untiring energy of the Rev W H Stables and his earnest band of workers. Be- sides this commendable enterprise .the rev gentle- man has shown much zeal and aggressiveness in in the matter of parochial organization, and it is doubtless his success in this direction which has commended him to the favourable consideration of the bishop in the appointment under notice.
