The Work of the Past Season.

Sir GILBERT GREENA.LL, Bart., presided over the annual meeting of tne Warrington and district Agricul- tural society which was held at the offices of Messrs. John White, Mclver aad Co., on Monday afteraoon. There were also present :—Messrs. H F Greenough (chairman of committee), J Hepnerd (vice-chairman of committee), Joseph Knowles (hon. treasuter), E Gibbon R Cldrke, E Clarke, J Mainwaring, W Fowles, jun., A Broome, James Gould, Joseph Gould, James Tickle, J Whitlow (Broomfields), J Whitlow; (PrPttoo Book), A Pimlott, I F. Taylor, J E Becker and H Beresford Mel- vine (secretary).

_ THE ACCOUNTS. The statement of accounts was presen!ed by the Secretary. The receipts including subscriptions, special prizes, ke., £230 4s 6d : special cionatioo, £250 ; entry tees for exhibits, £153 6s ; admission at gate and grand stand, £135 16e ; and other items making a total of £791 3s 3. Toe payments amounted to £767 lOs 4d ; and included the following items :—Prizes, £565 15s ; fitting itie show yard. £62 ; printing, advertising, &c., £5B 4s Si and judges' lees, salaries and commissions, £5B 7s 3d. The balance in hand on the year's working amounted to £23 12s 11d, which added to the balance in hand at the beginning of the year, makes the total amount to the credit of the society, £52 9s 91. The accounts were adopted. SECRETARY'S REPORT. The SECRETARY read the report as follows :—The twelfth annual show of the above society was held in Walton Park, by the kind permission of the President (Sir Gilbert Greenall. Bart), on Thursday, Bth August, 1901. At the annual meeting last year a revised set of rules for the more beaeficial working of the society was adopted, and the results so far have proved satisfactory The Vice-President for the year was Colonel Cornwall Legh, of High Legh, Knutsford.who has for many years supported the society. The large and much appreciated gathering of agriculturists at Sir Gilbert Greenall'a luncheon is another special feature of the stow. Apart from the show itself the many privileges accorded by the President to all visitors on tne show day in viewing his -iyate grounds and visiting his stables, ko, has created 00 society's annual show one of the recognized great days of the year in the locality. From the very first the members of the society have been indebted to Sir Gilbert for the generous support he has given them,both financially and in providisg a free ground and a most excellent ring. The entries were not so numerous as last year, but it was admitted that the quality of the exhibits keeps on insproviog, So much so that old ex- hibitors considered themselves so far below the average that they made few or no entries. For a local show this is to be regretted, and in some instances the entries. were obtained with much difficulty and considerably ' after the date published for the last day of entry., With regard to a day appointed for receiving' entries other measures will have to be . taken' If the lists bad been closed as announced it would not., have been desirable to bold the show as there were', very few entries. As a great deal ot clerical work has to be got through between the last day of entry and the day of the show it is a matter of the greatest ins— portance that the entries should be received as soon se Possible, and it is to be hoped in future that intending., exhibitors will bear this in mind. Ths shire classes" were considered good as regards the number of entries. There were, however, only a few barren mares in that particular class, as also yearling colts or geldings_ A. ' new class for two-year-old colts was added to the sche dule of prizes, but only four were entered. The drench gelding classes were not as good as last year, and again: there was the utmost difficulty in obtaining entries. The hunters, harness and hackney classes throughout were well filled, and the judges expressed themselves most, satisfied with the entries, some well-known exhibitors . . again taking prizes. Tne cattle classes still keep low in numbers, and it is considered necessary to make the classes more attractive to the neighbourhood. All the . produce classes were badly filled and a rearrangement of these might very well be considered. In the place or the silver challenge cup which was won outright at the 1900 show, the President now offers a £lO champion CUP each year ; the challenge cup for cattle -given by Mr Littleton Dewburat still remains the property of the society. The deficit in the account; is less thar. last year, and there are still hopes that the society may eventually be self-supporting. For a work of this kind there is no reason why the subscription list should not be (hulled, or at any rate very materially 141 increased, and if this could only be accomplished the _ committee would not have the necessity of asking the President each year for a " special donation," to meet the deficit. It was, hewever, expected that this year tnere would be a considerable reduction in the debit balance, especially if the weather had been settled. For some days, however, previous to the show there had been heavy rain, and on the show day there was a heavy downpeur in the middle of the dae which weakened the attenclanee. The prompt and systematic manner in which the stewards carried out their part of the work was again a matter of the greatest satisfac- tion. It ma o not be generally known that a great deal of responsibility devolves upon the st ?wards on the day of the show, and they could easily make or mar the success of the day. The Secretary, in conclusion ' gave an analysis of receipts for the last six years. In 1896 the total teceints were £309 Is ; 1897, £259 4s ; 1898. £285 2; 61 ; 1899, £329 131 81 ; 1900, £464 2s 91 4111 and 1901, £533 16; 61; a grand total of £2,131 Os 5L . JONN VIVALKER AND CO., BiCIVSEY STREET, WARRINGTON YRISTM3 OF ALL DESCRINIONS WITH NEATNESS AND DESPATCH.

It appears, from the A Record Storm. meteorological reports, that the gale of the 12th just, which brought down the telegraph wires, cutting off a great part of North and West Britain from all rapid exchange of intelligence with other parts of the kingdom, and threatening to leave the world in ignorance of the great Chesterfied oration of Lord ROSEBERY, was a record storm in its way. It seems that it takes a rare combination of unto- ward weather to bring about such a disaster to telegraphic communication. Gales, and even hurri- canes, alone will not suffice ; the keen wires, if free from superincurnbrance, are proof even against fierce cyclones, of which four or five visit these islands annually. Even the record blizzard of ,the century, on January 18th, 1881, or the Tay Bridge storm of December 28th, 1879, f ailed to accomplish all-round destruction to telegraphic communication. The first essential factor was the snow, which must be of special quality and condition—not the light crisp, feathery article one sees on Christmas cards but not at Christmas ; sleety snow, in the melting mood, which, as so frequently happens, is decep- tive, for it no sooner touches the wire than it freezes and adheres, gathering in thickness and in weight as tho sleety hours pass. Should the operation cantinue long elough, and be accom- panied by a gradual fall of the surrounding tem- perature to freezing point, then the wires get into prime condition for the approaching storm,which is to sweep the puny works of inere man to destruction. They become the nucleus of long, pallid streaks of solid ice, horizontal icicles stretch- ing for miles and miles, reaching a thickness of as much as three inches, with an ornamentation of vertical pendants at intervals. In this condition, strained and overladen with the unusal weight of 11b. per foot, the gale finds them, and makes splendid havoc along nes which, once begun, is easily carried to amazing lengths, which are limited only by the absence of the conditions stated. In the gale of the 12th the unfavourable conditions pre- vailed across the Midlands, and thence northwards so that the consequent disaster was very general. Fur such mischief there is apparently no remedy so long as overhead wires are used. These are superior for short vibrations, and, therefore, for telephonic and quick telegraphic work, to wires laid underground, which are aff3cted by earth currents, no matter how well insulated. The remedy used in Germany is a duplicate set of wires underground,which are applicable both for strategi- cal purposes under Government and for taking the place of the overhead wires when these are broken or deranged by storm or other acccident. To adopt this method over the field of our Post Office operations would mean an enormous expense, which the Department are not likely to be willing to face for a considerable time to come. By the time they are in a position to do more than con- template it, perhaps the war will be over, and by that time possibly Signor IVIARCONI may have Isomething more to say upon the subject.

The Nemesis that The Telephone Charges. pursues slackness and extravagance, an d which generally overtakes its victims, is hard on the heels of the Government Tee Postmister - General has had no peace since he unwillingly gave to the public the agreement he has made with the National Telephone Campany. Not that the withholding of the documents would have made any difference, for L erd LONDONDERRY'S hand was forced, and the contents of the agree- ment were already known ta the pa olic through the:medium of the Press before the,autherised text appeared in print. His lordship has been so much the victim or the fellow-conspirator of the National Telephone Company against the public weal that the agreement has been produced as a completed instrument, signed, sealed, and effective which cannot be gone back upon and cannot be altered till 1905. But the public refuse to accept this view of the situation, and have gradually been gathering their forces with the intention of giving effect to this view. When the City of London unites with the County Council to con- elemn the action of a Government department, that dep irtrn ant and its chief are in a perilous position, and the Government itself is not safe. Not only is this so, but the conference which the LORD MAYOR of London welcomed to the Guild- hall consisted of two hundred and thirty-eight delegates who represented eighty-one local authorities, governing a superficial area of six hun.dred and thirty-four squ ire miles with a popu- lation of six millions,and a rateable value of nearly fifty millions. This unispe demonstration of all the interests had one further feature of importance. In a long and able debate, in which every form of argument was used to discredit the action of the Government in the matter of the teleptione nego- tiations, only a solitary voice was raised in pallia- tion of the Government position ; and the resolution which declared for a real, active competition, protested against the Past- master's scale of charges, and expressed regret that he did not consult the despised local authorities before practically giving himself away. That resolution was unanimously received and adopted. That is a demonstratioa of public opin- ion which the Government cannot well afford to disregard, and yet it places them in a very awk- ward corner. In 189rP Mr HANBURY told the House of Commons that the Government meant to" popularise the telephone system, and throw it open to the old country, to ricl, and poor alike:, These infrequent and spasmodic gushes of demo- cratic sentiment by Conservative Ministers are always suspicious, frequently ominous. The fol- lowing year found Mr. flaNeunv tossed on to the Board of Agriculture to tend the turnips and live stock of the nation, Having rid themselves of an inconvenient Minister, the Government seem to have thought they were free from the policy he had announced. Under their favourite method of government by departments, a little shuffling of the cards was all that was necessary to justify a change of attitude from competition to co-opera- tion, and from the democratisation of the tele- phone to something quite the reverse. It has been the Government's crowning effort in their pet hobby of endowing "private enterprise." It may seem strange that those who are so in love with the hobby when it takes the form of water or trams should so seriously object to it in 'phones. But the relation of mum, et hum, is still a power- ful factor in determining the attitude of the ordinary citizen; and when the City man finds, as one of them said at the Guildhall, that an article like the 'phone, which not only affects his business, but enters into the comforts of his daily lie, is deliberately kept up in, price—ah ! then the case is different.

There will be general concurrence with the view expressed by the chairman at the meeting of schoolmasters at Cambridge University, that there is great danger of the question of secondary education slipping into the vortex of party politics. There could be nothing more undesir- able, and it would be well if the Government learnt wisdom before it is too late, and made up their minds to put forward the matter in an un- ' contentious form. It is desired on all sides that ' secondary education should be dealt with; the subject is very much in arrear, and there is no further time to be lost. Why, then, should it be encumbered with the large questions of national education, the suppressing or superseding of the school building, and., the future of primary edu- cation ? It is certain that this wide field of opera- tions cannot be covered satisfactorily in one measure. It is well that members on both sides of the House of Commons should be requested to deal with secondary education as a purely non- party question, which it essentially is; but how can they honestly give such a pledge, if the whole of the principles underlying our national system of education are to be plucked up by the roots, and others substituted therefor ? It is evident that the first duty lies with the Government, who ought to bring in a Bill dealing with secondary ' education alone. This is all they have any chance of passing, and to attempt more will only lead to the whole legislation on the subject being post- poned for another year.

The trade-union workmen, who have been twitted in the Press for their unreasonable opposi- tion to new machinery and labour-saving contriv- ances, will now be able to retort that this phase of unreason is no confined to their class, but is shared by the bloated capitalist. No sooner has word come that wireless telegraphy has been tested, and found to answer for transmission of at least one letter across the Atlantic, than the ominous intelligence comes to hand that the experiments of the inveLtor have been interdicted by the Anglo-American Cable Company. who con- sider that Mr. Marconi is poaching on their pre- serves. The company, it seems, have a monopoly of tho telegraph service, under an agreement which terminates in 1904 ; but notwithstanding the near approach of that—perhaps in consequence of it— they object to allow the operations to proceed at St. 'John's. It is true that the operations are only in the experimental stage, and that the results are as important to the science of the world as to the interests of a particular syndicate; but the corn- pany will have none of it. Dog-in-the-manger like, they will not allow Marconi to go on alone, nor do they oiler to join him in the enterprise. The ground:is theirs and they warn him off. For- tunately Mr. Marconi is a sensible man as well as a skilful inventor. He will not seek to dispute the company's claim—which might be disputed. He will carry off his instruments elsewhere, prob- ably to Nova Scotia, where the experiments will be continued. In 1904 probably the Anglo• American Company will find in him a powerful competitor for the conveyance of transatlantic telegrams. We make progress fast in these days.

There are few savings banks in the country which have made such steady and continuous pro- gress as the Warrington Savings Bank. Founded so long ago as 1818, the bank has had an un- interrupted career of success and at the present moment the number of depositors reaches the satisfactory total of 12,894 with 25,228 transac- tions during the past year. Such a large number of depositors—exceeding one- fifth of the entire population of Warrington— hears ample testimony of the thriftiness of the, poople in this district. A feature of the past year's business has been the large amount of Govern- ment stock bought for depositors amounting in the aggregate to £3,736, which is the largest amount of stock bought in any single year since the bank was established. In addition, there are a number of depositors who have invested their savings in Warrington Corporation securities. Of the stability of the bank there can be no question. The amount due to depositots is £352,323 6s 3d, and the whole of this amount is covered by actual deposits with the National Debt Commissioners. In addition, the bank has a substantial sum in the hands of the treasurer as well as a valuable asset in the value of the bank premises which are freehold.

MATTERS OF MOMENT. A Provincial Letter.

The great festival is past. From the joys of Christmas we shall in three more days have turned to the welcoming in of the New Year. The ring- ing of church bells will once more be heard in the provinces, varied by the music of bands playing the time-honoured tune of Auld Lang Syne ; and the birth of the year 1902 will be duly heraldel as were all its predecessors for a period as far back as any of us can recall. And assuredly the pleasing sentiment, and the bright hopes that are signified I by the church bells, and Oa endeared scotch song, I are both natural and appropriate. It is well at the present moment to reit,ember, too, that one basis of the feeling of goodwill at Christmas-tide is the sense of duty discharged, not grudgingly but with the glee that arises from a warm heart. Our newspaper columns to-day show, as they have so often done before, that if the well-to-do have feasted, so also have the poor. It can be said for the rest of mankind that at this season the very poor among us are never forgotten. Not only the more or less public institutions, but many pri- vate instrumentalities are brought into operation for the help and solace of the suffering and the un- fortunate. The latter means are nose the less effective and useful for being silent and unobtru- sive, but in many instances are for these reasons all the more acceptable and opportune. They wear at Christmas the steady glow of specially active sympathy with those who share little or not at all in this world's gear. It is, indeed, through these private and unpretentious agencies that we get B fuller glimpse than at any other time of what the " open hand," whether the right or left, does in the shape of the twice blessed gift of real charity. Yet it is but a portion, and perhaps a comparatively small one, of that which is done at this period of the year through the many cb.hannels to which all classes contribute, and so help to realise the poet's ideal of the whale' brothsrhood of man,"

New Year's Day generally brings with it certain new laws, or amendments of existing laws, and ' on Wednesday next the Factory and Workshops Act of the present year, and likewise the Youthful Offenders' Act, will come into operation. Tus former is of considerable importance to all persons in the country coacerned in manufactures, work- shops, and business enterprises in which numbers of persons are employed. It is also of interest to members of the various municipal councils. One of the changes in the law to be observed by Dis- trict Councils is, that if they fail in their duties the Home Office may cause an inspection to be made as being authorised to enforce the provitions of the Act, District Councils may make bye-laws to provide means of escape from tire, and generally regarded, there is in the Act an attempt to prevent accidents by measures of prec iution, instead of by merely punishing offenders when such accidents occur. An important alteration is the raising of the age of protection for children to twelve years, but probably the most drastic Tchange is in the large powers given, with certain safeguards to the Home Secretary for the control of dangerous trades. In sections 79 and 80 the powers of the Home Office to make regulations are enormously increased, SO much so. indeed, that the Secretary of State may practically declare what trades are dangerous or unhea thy,and henceforward very much will depend in fact upon the decisions of the department. The regulations give it the power to limit, prohibit, or to control the use of any particular material, or any process of business considered by it to be dan- gerous or unhealthy; and the Act renders liable any occupier of a facto-y, or his contractor, if he allows clothing to be made by outworkera where there is small-pox or scarlet fever, while section 100 gives the District Council power to prohibit homework where th ere is infectious disease.

I.ln regard to the Youthful Offenders' Act, it may be said that this is one of those useful pieces of legislation which have for their object the care of young persons in danger of becoming criminals, either by wrong-doing or the neglect of their parents and guardians, or by the operation of laws which unnecessarily send them to prison. It is a short Act, providing that any coart of summary jurisdiction, on remanding or committing a child or young person may. instead of committing him to prison, remand or commit him into the custody of any fit person named in the commitment who is willing to receive him—" due regard being had to the religious persuasion of the child"—to be so detained until thence delivered by due course of law. The Act was passed in August and local authorities in many parts dale provinc,.B are now registering names and addressesl of " fit persons" to he entrusted with the care of remanded or committed young people. It is agreeable to find chat some of the offences a conviction of which has hitherto been allowed to stamp a child as a felon are to do so no long-r.

Underground telegraphy is a subject which hasbeen brought: very prominently before the public, par- ticularly in the northern provinces,by recent!stcrms The Postmaster-General, having been memorial- ised in reference thereto, holds out as little hope of any effective measure being adopted by his depart- ment, as he did when waited upon by an influential delegation on the telephone charges question. But in this case Ltd L3ndonderry has a little more reason on his side. For it is generally remnised that there are financial and tec-mical difficulties in the way of burying the wires between important districts at the present juncture, and that people in the provinces have to content themselves as best they can with the Postmaster's assurance that the dPpartment will keep the suggested sch •me for , underground wires constantly before them, in order , that, where posible, such wires may be laid down, ' A public agitation in favour of a complete system of underground wires would a year or two ago have been carried on with vigour and earnestness; but within the past few weeks events have happened ' that may give Pause to the more enthusiastic advo- cates of the disestablishment of overhead wire. The Post Office authorities are probably not blind , to the possibilities that have been opened up by the latest triumph of the Marconi system of wireless telegraphy, which may at some future date be used for the transmission of inland messages, as well as for transmarine inter-communication. The action of the Anglo-American Cable Company in enforcing its right to stop Marconi's experiments in New- foundland may be accepted as conclusive proof that transatlantic wireless telegraphy is not regarded by practical men as a mere scientific plaything. Whether the Company reconsider their decision or not, these things point to the anxiety there is to re cognise wireless telegraphy as one of the great forces of the future ; and the general attitude un- doubtedly affects that of the British Post Office towards the new discovery, and has brought to bear upon the authorities an amount of restraining influence which they will not find it very difficult to justify.

The sequel to the late Grimsby fishermerfa dis- pute,which lasted so long and became so embittered has been furnished this week in an award by the arbitrator, Bir Edward Fry, which is substantially a victory for the men. All the new conditions im- posed by the Owners' Federation are discarded by the arbitrator, who decides that the old conditions shall be resorted to. The decision will no doubt occasion surprise to those who believed that the owners occupied a practically unassailable position. The owners' arguments were said at one time to be powerfully supported by the entries in their books, which were freely submitted to the examination of impartial witnesses during the progress of the dis- pute. Sir Edward Fry's decision, however, after I careful examination of the case from both sides, , quite upsets this conclusion, and suggests that the ' responsibility for the temporary dislocation of the Grimsby fishing trade rests upon those who acted as the advisers of the Owners Federation. it is not often that predictions as to the ultimate issue of an industrial conflict are so completely falsified as they have been in this ease. The full text of the award in this singularly complicated trade dispute must be awaited with unusual interest by many students of domestic economy.

LECTURE ON " KEATS " BY MR. W. DOUGLAS.

On Friday evening at the dllesmere-etreet Debat- ing Society, a lecture was given by Mr W Douglas on" Keats." The lecturer gave a brief biography of the young poet's life and touched with particular emphasis on his romantic love and courtship. His love letters, he contended, were among the choicest of literary writings, and were too sacred to be sub- jected to indiscriminate criticism. A vote of thanks to Mr Douglas for his lecture brought the meeting to a close.

CRUELTY TO Cix LDRE:k7 AT WIDNES.

At the Widnes Police Court, on Monday, Julia Fowler, of 2, fligh-street, Wic!nos, was charged fl 3 the custodian of four children with neglecting them.—The police stated that visits had ben paid to the house, and the woman bad apparently no visible means of subsistence. On one visit three men, two of them being supposedly respectable married men, were found in the back yard. The house was always dirty, and devoid of furniture ; the woman was addicted to drink, and the chil- dren, aged respectively, 2,3, 6, and 11, were often left to run about almost naked.—tt was alo stated that the police had had to find clothes for the youngsters, and that the out-relief given by the Guardian, of the Poor to the women had been stopped on account of continued drankennes-i.- -The Bench sentenced the woman to a month's hard labour, and directed that the children should be taken to the workhouse, with a view to their adoption by the guardians.

W. Timmins (in the chair) and R. H. Posnett, William and Mary Ellen Clarke were charged with having neglected their six children in a manner likely to cause unnecessary suffering.— The evidence submitted by Inspector Hackett and corroborated by Sergeants Brewster and Gregory, showed that the prisoners' house in Bridgewater-street, where they had lately resided, was in a shockingly dirty condition. When they removed the female prisoner stated that she was going on tramp with the children and their whereabouts were not ascertained until Friday when it was found that they were residing in Shaw-street. Sergeant Gregory scaled the wall and afterwards let the other witnesses in. The sight was an appalling one The children were almost naked and very dirty and the woman herself was in a most filthy condition. The fire was a pretty good one but there was no food in the house save a quarter of bread. There was no furniture and the children slept on a flock bed on the floor.—The Chairman de.cribed the case as one of the worst he had ever known in the whole of his experience. He had seen the children in the police cells and the eight wa most heart rending. The man was getting £1 13s per week of whicn .£1 went to his wife end the home affairs should have been conducted differently. The man would be sentenced to one month imprisonment and the woman to three montns.

CHRISTMAS AT RUNCORN.

For another twelve months Christmas is a thing of the past. It cannot be said that Christmas weather prevailed right through ClirVcmas week but the atmospheric conditions, on the whole were quite in harmony with what one expects on this occasion. Prior to Cnristmas Eve so keen a frost prevailed that it was with difficulty ped,strians could walk the streets, while horse traffic was brought, literally to a standstill. Christmas Eve and Christmas Day witnessed, however, quite a change. A sharp thaw sat in which quite spoiled the anticipated skating at the Runcorn Park. Nevertheless a few daring youths made a vigor- ous attempt to cut figures on the ice with the result that they cut different figures in the water quite opposite to their expec- tations—and sorry figures they were too. Happily nothing worse resulted than the pleasure of steno- , tog three feet deep in ice cold water. Skating for all practical purposes was completely spoiled. On show night toe frost was keen and cutting. and made the well lighted shops look still more brilliant, packed as they were, with their new Christmas goods and novelties. Generally there is a feeling among tradesmen that there is a distinct falling off in the purchase of "Christmas Joys" compared with previous years. To a certain extent this may be due to the fact tbat many works did not pay their men on Christmas Eve but deferred payment until the end of the week. As is usual Christmas Eve saw tbe usual number of brass bands and parties of Christmas singers. iany commenced operations early in:the evening and did not finish until day broke on another Christmas Day, At most of the places of worship services were held and , there were also parties and social gatherings held in connection with them. Very few went out of town, the London and North-Western Rail- way station being closed, most people, preferring to spend their Christmas at home. Boxing day was mild and pleasant. Many people journeyed out of town to Liverpool and other places where facilities were cffared for amusement. Christmas, it may be said, was spent in much the same fashion as in previous years, and, on the whole, doubt- less it was spent with as much joviality as in the larger towns.

SA.LVATION ARMY CHRISTMAS TEA. On Ciaristmis Day the Army held a tea meeting, at which over one hundred persons were prsseat. Captaitt Yoang superintended operation., and the tea was a cm,pl-te suczes3: Thanks are due to the donors who so liberally subscribsd. Sir John T Brunner, as usual, was to the front with a donation of three guineas. Addresses were afterwards given.

RAILWAY FATALITY AT WIDNES.

Old Footballer Killed.

- -...--. On Christmas Eve the mutilated body of Frederick Plumpton, of Hough Green, near Widnes, was bind on the Cheshire Lines Railway near the Ball of Dittan be- tween the Widnes and H nigh Green stations. The deceased had been a been local sportsman, and at one time was captain of the Widnes footbel team. He also played boa Warrington, and was very well- known in bon towns. He was only abut thirty years of age. He leaves a widow, woo is the daughter of Mr LI Dale the wel;-khowin cricketer. The facts have been reported to the coroner, and an inquiry will be held.

Warrington added one moLe toßuncorn's growing list of defeats on Thursday at lrwsli-lane, which was a bitter pill for the locals to swallow.

The start was twenty-five minutes after time') owing, it is stated to Mr Priestly, the referee, being late, and when the teams entered the enclosure the crowd was surprised to find that the Linnets had doffed their green jerseys and arrayed themselves in white. Sudden Death in Sankey-street. The sudden death is reported of Mr Bradshaw who was for a considerable number of years manager of the public baths in Legh-etreet. He I was walking along Sankey-street on Sunday evening, at about nine o'clock, when be was seen to stagger and fall to the ground. He wa4 picked up and taken to the Infirmary, where it was found that life was extinct. Death of Mr. William Grimshaw. On Monday morning, at Rocktield, Grappenhall- after a painful illness, borne with Christian forti tude, Mr William Grimehaw passed away. Residing near W arrington for many years, he was well- known to a large circle of friends, and highly esteemed for his strict integrity and kindliness of heart. Mr Grimehaw was for many years cashier, and afterwards a director of Mackie and Clrcpany Limited. .

Harriers.

The Sankey club held-iheir-annual Christmas handi- cap on Saturday afternoon, the run taking place from the Sankey schoolroom. Notwithstanding the bitterly cold weather, and the frosty state of the ground, twenty-three out of the twenty-five entrants put in an appearance. No trail was laid, a road race of fully seven miles having been decided upon. The limit lads received • start at the rate of a minute per mile. Twenty-one prizes were offered, and a number of con- solation prizes were afterwards given. Joseph Riley was first to make an appearance, and after a very fast race succeeded in getting home in front of T Devlin by about 100 yards, the same distance separating second and third. The times and order of finishing of the first six were :—J. Riley (21min.), 1 ; T. Devlin (scratch), 2 ; J. Potts.(2l min.), 3 ; F. Cowap, (2± min), 4 ; D. Green (imin.), 5 ; S. Parsonage ( min.), 6. The winner's time for the whole distance was 441 minutes, this being easily beaten by Devlin, the second man, whose time was 42 minutes. The handicappers are to be complimented on their ex- cellent work, the various starts giving every satisfac- tion. There is Security in I A the'

smile flooded the chamber. No sound was heard save the subdtied murmur from the streets below —no sound save the intermittent sob of the depart- ing spirit. The old man sat motionless. He heard the faint breathing—"the wave of life ebbing to and fro," and how the spells of bilence each time became longer. But he beheld not. how suddenly the child's eyes opened and fixed themselves upon his unconscious face! She cast her regards aloft with a smile. At the same instant her breath ceased. The mist bad pused from the star ! Now, explain it as you will, but at that very moment of her departure, the old man too followed her glance upward. His restless hand became motionless on the divine page. lie murmured the name of his darlinp as though he beheld her face at last. Thep his breath also ceased and their clasped hands grew cold together. So they were found, hours afterward, in the dusk of the night, their faces the same in unspeakable bliss—their eyes fixed upon the same spot above ! With tears the icy page was seen, and the old man's icy finger pointing to the words—" For Thou wilt light my candle : the Lord my (God will enlighten my darkness." such is the simple story of the blind Scripture reader and his little grand-child whose faces you may see in the beautiful picture of Dyckma

'IHE EXAII.IINER FRII)AY, DECEMBER 27 1901

IHE BLIND SCRIPTURE READER.

[By W. H. Leathwood, Author of Tales of Many Colours &c.]

Have you ever seen The Blind Beggar of Dyck- mans—have you seen the beautiful picture ? It represents an old man of commanding stature of venerable appearance, whose snow-white beard sweeps down upon his breast—whose face and sightless eyes are raised to heaven. Shrinking at his side and clasping his hands is a fragile girl— delicate,golden-haired,and beautiful. Her attitude is full of grace like a bending flower, but it is the grace of childhood of weakness of dependence ! Her brow is madonna-like.her mouth mournful and sweet—the curve of her neck beautiful. But it is the child's unfathomable eyes which are altogether indescribable. Their expression as they look ' straight from the canvas is like the glance of Reynolds's angels—incomprehensibly deep as though fixed upon unearthly mysteries. They are filled not with sadness and solemnity, but with the spirit of these, too deep for words to describe, Un- like the old man whose blindne4s is the shade of the Almighty, she looks out upon the world but in her regards too is that Almighty shadow which, like Death the gate of Life, seems only a thin film through which we discern her soul and heaven. The old man is blind—the child almost blind to the world about her. They are one in their spirit, in their helplessness. Youth and purity side by side with age and blindness. Could anything be more pathetic? As we gaze at the picture we seem to hear the tumult of the world about them yet also we discern the Divinity which hedge; them round and through whose still radiance no evil can pierce to hurt their souls !

Now this description of Dyckmans' picture is the very portraiture of the blind Scripture-reader and his lictle grandchild whose story I am about to relate—though to most people it may not be much of a story after all. There was the same majesty of form and face in the old man, the same ineffable sweetness iu the child. They were alone in the world which is so great—they were helpless where all are so hard and cold and heedless.

The place where the old man used to sit during the afternoons and late into the evenings was by the wall of the big city cemetery, which for some distance forms one side of the wide street. Here be had a low stool and when the rain fell fast a big umbrella. lie had also a thick waterproof cape. But these things did not prevent him getting very wet sometimes, nor keep the bitter cold from chill- ing his blood and stiffening his ageil limbs. During the bitter winter days, when the icicles lay stiff upon his beard the pallor of his face becams such that face and beard were her ily distinguish- able. Of course it was only in the summer time that his grandchild was with him or in the very warmest days of winter. At other times he sat alone thinking about her. When he was not read- in :z he would continue in this way for long spells, silent with his eyes fixed on vacancy. sometimes, at such a moment, when he was troubled to know whether she was in safety his lips moved awl then you heard faintly the deep prayer—" Lord, rescue my soul from their destruction. And my darling from the lions I" _

Here, then was his place. day by day, by the wall of the great cemetery. Tnere were many passers- by and much traffic, for, though the street was mostly composed ol private houses, it connected two great thoroughfares of the city. Behind him was that great silence of the dead with which he so often communed. lie was a familiar object to most eyes. They glanced at him carelessly and passed on The bright sunlight, the noise and con- fusion, the varied sights and sounds, the hours of labour and of business, all these conspire to close the souls of men and to render their minds heedless of poverty and misfortune. And the old man felt this instinctively. During the daylight hours he seldom read, but sat silent as I have said—s mysterious temple of meditation—in the midst of the busy throng. Sometimes, at rare intervals, a hand wool I steal out of the tumult, touch his own with a small offering and immediately pass away into the night which surrounded him ! Then the old man would start with a blessing and sit ponder- ing for long after trying in his own mind to picture the giver that he might know it again in eternity. " It was a good bean," he would say," or a suffer- ing one, for none other in this moving life would see the old man."

The doge, however, ulnae frequently, and licked his hand.

But when night drew on apace, and the day de- parted, and darkness with its mystery settled upon the busy streets, and the pas-era-by became fewer and fewer, and business was at an end for one day more,—and men, with the depar- ture of light became more thoughtful and serious then the old man's work day began and he lifted up his voice in the stillness That wonderful voice of the Scripture-reader—it ' was the fitting expression in depth and volume— of his noble counten ince, his lofty presence 1 More than this it was the voice of his very soul, that soul, which deprived of the garish light of the world sat daily in the presence of Him Who has covered Himself with light as with a garment. Who sows light for the righteous and Who makes His face to shine upon His servant! It was a voice of great manhood, which, in Fin Oriental city, would have made of him a muezzin to chant the glory of Allah and of the Prophet. It was a voice which had the additional power, if we may say so, of his lost sight. For the eye speaks—the eye has a langu- age, but the eye of the Scripture-reader had never spoken. That is what we mean when we say that his voice had that additional power of his lost sight. It was the one and on!yr expression of the old man's coal!

Imagine this wonderful voice, by night, rising from the dark wall of the cemetery. Where he eat there was no lamp for at least a dozen yards for be needel no light to read his book of Psalms. If you walked along the pavement quite close to him you beheld his dim form in tue gio ma but to pass- ers-by on the opposite side he was merely a Voice like that, voice in the wilderness of old. To those who knew of the cemetery being there it was addi- tionally solemn; and though many, even at night, would pass heedlessly there were deeper natures whom his voice arrested—who stood breathlessly night after night and li•tened as to a voice from the dead. " What man is he that liveth and shall not see death 7 Shall he deliver his soul from the hand of the grave?"

I have said that only in summer and the warmes days did the old man's grandchild sit with him She was very delicate. Bat she suffered no pain— she experienced no ill-health as we understand the word. It was simply that her heavenly spirit had been clothed with an ever-wesikening body of th frailest texture which would presently fade from her like a thin mist from a star, leaving it to shine at the time appointed. Looking at this beautiful child it was easy to believe in an immaculate incar- nation ! Sin had never touched her. Communion with the world she had never known, Life passed before her eyes only like a troubled dream. She beheld it ever with a gentle sorrow, as something which came between her and Paradise—as some- thing which would inevitably pass away, leaving her sight bright and clear. With every recent year of her life she had become more spiritual—with every year her strength decreased ! Her face be- came thinner and mere ethereal ! Her eyes were more unfathomable !N When she was about fourteen she lay down on her bed nicer to rise again ! It was the mist at length passing from the star! They told the old man she would soon die and be ' said nothing. But now, during the day, he never left her, only at night-time did he stAal forth for an hour and sit by the wall of the cemetery. What his thoughts were none could tell. He never spoke nor read, His voice had been heard for the last time in that busy street—and his eyes had no language. With his dumb look fixed on vacancy, motionless, as one dead,his book on his knee, he sat. To see him thus one might have fancied that life had already departed, but that, every now and then, he started and passed his hand weirdly across the mystic pages. He was groping in the dark for the fingers of God! _

At length, with the decline of a summer day, the child lay dying. The old man sat by her side, his book on his knee, one hand moving across the open page as usual, the other clasp- ing her thin fingers. The sun, silent and glorious, looked from the west. His breathless

OUR LOCAL CHURCHES.

Cairo-Street Chapel.

On Sunday evening the Rev E W Lummis, pastor of the above place of worship continued his discourses on Unitarianism, raking for his subject on this occasion A Unitarian's thought of Christ." The sermon was istened to with interest by & fairly large congregation.

===== 0002601_19011227_art0096.txt | included=true | included =====
Golborne-Street Baptist Chapel.

Large congregation s assembled at the above place of worship, on Sunday, when sermons were preached, morning and evening, by Mr Herbert Dunnico, of Bolton In the morning the sermon was necessarily short the Dreaoher having been delayed on the railway on his journey from Bolton, and the service was partly over when he arrived at the chapel. Special Christmas hymns and carols were heartily sung, the collections realizing a satisfactory sum.

Legh-street Primitive Methodist Chapel.

On Sunday, at this chapel, the choir. assisted by friends, gave selections fro in Handel'; " Messiah." The chapel was filled to overdo wing, and the service was heartily enjoyed by all present. The principals were Miss Alice Bridge, Miss Eva Miller, Mr John Corrin, and Mr E H Pearcs, A.L.C.M., Miss Bridge, who is the possessor of a fine soprano voice, sang with great fer- vour the beautiful soles and recite, "There were Shepherds " "Rejoice greatly" " Come unto Him " and " How beautiful are the feet." She undoubtedly sang the solo. " Rejoice greatly " the best, and must be con- gratulat on vie aimless she has attained. Miss Eva Miller sang the recit and aria " He shall feed his flock," very effectively, and her sweat mezzo- soprano voice was especially suited to the soh, alloted: to her. Mr Jshn Corrin sane the recit " Comfort yo " and the aria " Every valley," the recit and aria "Inc was out off" and " But Thou didat not leave" in whicii his pure tenor voice was heard to advantaqe. The choir has on former occasions sl•eady made a name for the attack and precisi In with which they deal with difficult choruses, and they sang the choruses " And the glory " " Oh thou that tellest " For unto us a child is born " Glary to God," " Behold the Lamb of God " " All we like sheep " " Worthy is the lamb " " Hallelujah." Ordination of Deacons at Warrington.

The Lord Bishop of Liverpool hold an ordination of deacons at the Warrington Parish Church on Sunday morning.:lt is a considerable time 0003 a similar aervice took place at Warrington, but, no doubt owing to the intensely cold weather, the congregation was not a large one. The order of service set forth in the Prayer Book was gone through, commencing with the sermon which was preached by the Rector (Bev Canon Willis.) The rev. gentleman took for his text "And all that sat in council looking steadfastly on him saw his face as it had been the face of an angel. And he kneeled down and cried with a loud voice, Lord, lay not thia sin to their charge. And whea be bad said this he fell asleep." The Rector said that such was the appearance and such was the death of the first and greatest of the deacons— St. Stephen. He proceeded to recall the circumetances of the appointment alai death of St. Stephen and con- tinuing said that looking back for nearly two thousand years they could find that from that time to the present there had always been the order of deacon. The Rector now spoke upon the office and duties of the deacons and of the estimation in which they should be held by the people. am asked his hearers not to despise nor yet to flatter the deacons but to help them by kind appreciation of their efforts and also by prayer.—The candidates having been I presented by the Bishop the Litany was sung and was followed by a celebration of Holy Conainunion, tne ordination taking place after the reading of the Epistle. The following candidates were duly presented and ordained :—Rebert Henry Barclay, B A, Wadbam College, Oxford, licensed to Seaforth ; David. Rhys Jardei, of the London College of Divinity, St Johns's Hall, Highbury, Reprised to St Peter's, Warrington ; Frederic Herbert La Trobe, B A, of Owens College, Manchester, in Victoria University licensed to Sutton ; Harold Arthur Holt Lee, B A, of Emmanuel College, Cambridge, licensed to St Nsthaaiel's Windsor, West Derby. For the colonien under commission from the Archbishop of Canterbury; Gilbert Harding, of St Bonif ace Missionary College, Warminster.The Gospel was read by the Rev El A H. Lee newly appointed deacon. The Bishop er as attended by the examining chaplains, the Rev H G Gray, M A, Principal of Wycliffe Hall,Oxford (who presented the candidates;)and the Rev. Morley Stevenson,M;A,Principal of the Warrington Training College ; and the Rev H E H Protya, MA, domestic chaplin. The service was a very impressive one, the choir rendering the choral positiens most creditably. Mr F M Darby pre- sided at the organ. . . .

In the evening the Bishop preached to a large congre. gation from the words : " Watch ye and keep them 1

until ye weigh them at Jerusalem, in the chambers of the house of the Lord,' Ezra. viii. 23 After speaking:of I trio treasure which had been committed to the five dea- cons who had been ordained that thy, his lordohip said to-Jay it was equally true of every age that a storm was raging round the Word of God, but they need have no tsar of the result. As Bishop Westcott once well said, " The Bible wil. take care of itself." Tnis was not the first storm to which it had been exposed, and it would net he the last. Some present were old enough to reine:nber the terrible onslaught made Ilion the New Testament by a singularly gifted and in tt-llec tual set of German scholars. They would remember how the faith of men even in this land was staggered. Had the New Testarneut lost any of its power ? No, and Be of whom it spoke seemed to stand out before the eyes of men more real, and vivid, and clear, than ever He did before. The, New TesLa,ment had passed through , the tire and had lost nothing, and so it would be with Books of the Old Testament. It might he that on some , points they might have to alter preconceived notions which were not justified by the Word of God, but which were simply the creation of a pious imagination or of enthusiastic devotion ;but of this they might be quite sure, that when criticism had done its very worst, and had spoken its last words, the children who would come after them would own the Books of the 01,1 Testament as they owned the Books of the New Testament, as indeed the word of the livieg God. Allusion was made by his lordship to the dangers which lay before deacons, against , which they had to be on the watcb—inoonsistency, unreality, and indolence. It was inconsisteney which , ruined the influence of many a clergyman and lay worker. Chriitians were men who were watched, and if the world saw that they professed one thing with the lip and acted contrary in the life, they laughed at religion and the Maeter was scorned. Unreality took the heart out of their message,and to-day the Church of England was mouroiue because so comparatively tew men came forward an offered themselves for this hi, h office and holy work. Men told them that the cause Was that the payment was small, the truth was so uncer- tain, that worldly callings offered far higher induce- ments. He believed that God had scattered through t land hundreds of young men in the English Chore wham H,s voice was speaking, and whom His spirit was calling, and whet were waiting to have the way opened before them to the ministry. It was the duty of lay- men to look out good and fitting mon and do their beet to help them into the ministry, The Bishop alluded to the tact that one of the fiveldeacons was a towneman of their own, Mr Harding, who was going to Western .ss ustralia to carry the message of the Gospel.

A WARR!NGTON CABMAN'S DEATH

Collision with a Trolly Pole.

Mr Brighoure, county cvroner, conducted an inquiry in the Parade Room at the Police Station on Monday afternoon respecting the death of Sarnialf Bell, a cab- driver in Warrington for many yeare. On Saturday.the th inst, the deceased was driving a cab along Konts ford-road when the veaicle collided with an electric trolly pole. He was thrown to the ground and so badly injured that he died on the 19th inst., at the Infirmary to which piano he had been removed for treatment. Mr Browne appeared for Messrs Robert W Blackwell and Co, Ltd, the contractors for the erection of the trolley poles, Mr Stanley Wilsoo, deputy Town Clerk for the Corporation, and Mr Conncr for the relatives of the deceasei.

Sarah Bell, the widow of the deceased said her hus- band was 56 years of age and resided in Cairo-street. He came home at 11.30 p.m. on the Saturday in ques- tion bleedmg from a wound on the cheek. He appeared to be' stupefied and in a dazed condition." She asked him what was the matter with him, but he was unable to tell her. Be went to bed, but on the Sunday became worse. She sent for Dr Geary, who ordered the man's removal to the Infirmary, where he died at twenty minutes past ttiteN on Thursday last. . . . _

Richard Warburton, 'bus driver: said on Saturday, the 13th inst• he was driving towards Warrington along Knutsford-road when he passed deceased, who was driving a cab. While passing, deceased's cab caught a pole which had been recently erected for the new tram- way. There was no light on the pole although there were a number of other poles supplied with light. It was very dark at the time. In answer to the Coroner, Mr Browne stated that the poles were 40 yards apart. Witness said the deceased was on the near side, and there was a space of 9ft. be- tween the crolly pole and his side of the road. Be and deceased passed on the same side of the trolly pole. Two of the trolly poles had no lamps on at all. The near wheel of the cab caught the pole as far as witness could make out. The deceased could have come nearer to witness if he had liked. By Mr Coiaxoff : There was no one to direct the traffic or to point out danger. The Coroner : There was plenty of room to pass between the trolly pole and the parapet ?

Witness : Yes-, sir. The Coroner.: Where is the danger ?

„,_ Witness: We were bath passine at the time, and my 'bus would_take_the light from him.

James Ellis. of KuuCsford-road, said he was driving a 'lmo from Warrington to Latchford on the night in question when he saw that an accident had occurred. The deceased said that he had run into a pole which had no light on it, hut he did not appear to have been bort. Deceased went on witness's 'bus till they got to the terminus, whea he walked home.—By the Coroner : There would be danger if there was no light on the pole. There was no one about to see whether the lamps were kept lighted. There was a man higher .up the road to put thelamps en the poles at night. Henry Isherwood, in the employ of Messrs Blackwell and Company, said it was his duty :to clean the lamps, light them and put them on the poles. On the night in question he had not a lamp for every pole •, there were two short. He reported the matter to Mr \rouse, the representative of the firm. If the firm had supplied him with the lamps be would have put them up, but Mr %rouse said he could not find lamps. Mr BtOWLIE explained that the lamps were on their way from London and only came on Monday morning. Thl Coroner : If that is so that is a case for another Court, and it is not a question of criminal negligence. This is all we have to inquire into. Dr Mort, Ben., Rouse Surgeon at the Infirmary, gave evidence as to the cause of death.

_ William Vouse, a storekeeper in the service of Messrs Blackwell. said he came to Warrington before the trolly poles were, put up. Eleven were placed from the com- mencement of the widening of the road to Blackbear Bridge. T,.ey wanted 12 lamps, but could not get them from London. The lamps that were put on he got in Warrington. There were no danger lamps in Warring- ton. They were red 'danger lamps that had been ordered. Ile could only get plain white hurricane lamps. Be applied to Mr Ewarr. the tramways con- tractor, for lamps, but that gentleman hadn't one to spare. The danger lamps from London were the safes,. rho Coroner : If the lama Old been othere probably the accident would not have occurred,

Mr Brighouse then addressed the jury. and said be thought they were entitled to say that the se?.ident would not have ari.en if there had been a light on the Pole. and that any other question must be fought out elsewhere.

The jury were then left together to consider their verdict.

When the Coroner, witnesses, Szc., had been rc-ad- mitted, the Foreman said the jury were of opinion that the death was owina to an accident, but that it would not have occurred if there had been a lamp on this par- ticular pole.

EXTRAORDINARY INCIDENT. TRATICAR WITHOUT A DRIVER

AT T. HELENS.

There was an extraordinary imsident, at St. Helens on Saturday night in connection with the Derbyshire-hill car, which stated for Parr shortly b4ore eleven o'clock The car was proceeding at a great pace down Bro ad Oak-road into Chancery-lane, when, one of the passengers desiring to alight, the conductor gave the signal to stop, but this was disregarde d. The conductor on going to the front piatfrui found that the driver had disappeared, and the car ;was gaining speed and dashing along the road, which is at the best narrow and som,- what dangerous for traffic. The passengers hs- ammo greatly alarmed, and jumped up for the purpose of getting off the car, and in the struggle several people sustained cuts and bruises about the head and fans. The canductar stopped the car as it was approaching the Free Library at Parr Stocks, and told the pasiengets that if they would sit Still he would take them on to the tramway depot,whers they could report what bad happened. This was accordingly done, and later the passengers were taken home on the car by another driver. The conductor stated that the driver, John Heather, was on the car when it started, and he could not toil what had become of him. He was perfectly sober. Early on Sunday morning the tramway company's officials had heard nothing further of Heather, hut during the day it ^rams- pired that he had been picked up in a numbed and un- conscious condition by two colliers, and taken into a house near Parr Church. When the poor man revived he stated that he had become exceedingly cold and his hands were numb, and when passing through one of the loops where the cars pass the jerk of the car threw him out on to the road, and he received injuries to the Mead and face, and was for some time stunned and unable to move. It is also stated that another of the tramway drivers collapsed on Saturday afternoon when starting his car in Church-street. THE FREE CHURCHES I RAIL

CHRISTMAS SERVICES.

The number of Free Churches which 1 services on Christmas Day has increased of

years, and is increasing. We rejoice e:

at this, says a writer in the Free Cher Chronicle," for with Christmastide the hearts the people are peculiarly susceptible to ti appeals of the Gospel. Even the most worl, hardened men feel an unwonted kindliness al tenderness at this greatest and most joyo festival of the year. The case of Scrooge Dickens's Christmas Carol' contains an valuable lesson and suggestion to ministers the Gospel. Old Scrooge had been what' call a hard nail throughout the whole of ti year, but the gracious and. kindly influences the season had melted his hard old heart. WI can doubt that this was the time of g times when the Gospel of Christ might me easily have found entrance into his wither heart i'"

A " BOOMING " BOOK. Gipsy Smith's Autobiography; just publishei by the National Free Church Council, is enjoY ing a very great sale. The whole of the firs edition was Subscribed by the trade before th volume was ready. And as we write we learn tha' the second edition (the fourth thousand) also completely sold out. There is every hood that, with the exception of a few or. popular novels, this autobiography will be th book of the present publishing season. Gifis Smith has an interesting and romantic story t' tell, and by his missions to all parts of th country ho has won for himself an immeas and a very sympathetic public.

"UNCONSECRATED 7 OR "GENERAL." Those who heard Dr. Parker's famous Cronr well sermon will never forget the passage lei which he told of the account he had receive for the deepest grave he ever dug—an Bccollo, headed "'Unconsecrated Ground." The use 01 this term "unconsecrated" has always giv° great offence to Free Churchmen. A number of Free Church Councils have already secure the deletion of the word and the substitutiona " general " in its place, and many, others are pressing hard for this reform. Recently a col- respondent wrote to the British Weekly Or gesting that the Free Church Council shoula take the matter up. If the writer had made an thing like careful inquiry he would have discovered that the Free Church Council° had interested themselves in the subject lorg ago, and that where the reform has bee° obtained it has been almost invariabli through the instrumentality of the Councils. FLUTTERING THE DOVECOTS. Canon Henson, of Westminster Abbey, he,! created a great stir in the dovecots of the Chore.' of England. Fancy the doctrine of Apostchef Succession being denounced from the pulpit eo the Abbey! It is a staggering conception. this is what Canon Henson has beeivloing. has been saying what Nonconformists have b°ll, saying for a long time. Says the Cano,u "The doctrine of Apostolical Successo is based upon a foundation which crumblee at the touch of critical investigatiell If we could renounce that obstinatA fiction, we should at least have secured the external condition of Christian reunion. long as that barrier remains fraternity is a futile hope. The conception of a ministry succeedieg to the sacrificial functions, and perpetua.tnig: I the sacerdotal character of the Jewish priest' hood is utterly opposed to the apostolic coil' ception. The ministry of the Apostles was pre. eminently the ministry of the Word."

THE EDUCATION CAMPAIGN.

The most absorbing topic, next to the W' in the coming session of Parliament will be education. The Government ha s definiteli pledged itself to the clericai party so bring in bill. It is, at the present momeni, impossible to forecast the details of the measure, but tile spirit of the Government's educational polid has been made abundantly manifest by the bill on the subject that they have already passea", a spirit of hostility to School Boards and el• subservience to clericalism and sectarianisol' The National Free Church Council has issued manifesto on the subject, declaring for gni- versal School Boards, no tampering with the Cowper Temple clause, the creation of on° local and directly responsible authority ill areas of sufficient size for all grades of education, the abolition of religion 4 tests in schools and training colleges provided or maintained by public funds, and; adequate representation of the public on the managil bodies of Voluntary Schools. The Free Chare' Councils have set themselves the task of cease- less inculcation of these views upon tli° people. , One Federation has arranged fer no fewer than one hundred meetings. tention is that the Government shall clearl)(4, understand, before the bill is introduced, is the Mind of the people on the matter. THE FIRST MAN IN .SCOTLAND. Principal Rainy, of Edinburgh, who is 11 almost universal consent the first man in Scati land, though ho is very little known south ee the Tweed, has promised to take part in tilt, annual meetings of the National Free CharcP Council at Bradford next March. THE NORTHERN UNION.

THE NORTHERN LEAGUE.

• WARRINOTON V. HUDDERSFIELD. One of the few matches played on Saturday was that at Wilderspool where Huddersfield and Warringtoot were en- gaged. The visitors arrived half an hour late and only upon taking a vote among the players was it dealded to play, the ground being very hard in some places. Huddersfield were without Driver (forward) and Burgess took Townson's place at half beak in the Warrington ranks, the latter not having fulfilled all the regulationsias regards work. There were about 3,000 spectators present. Mr Benton of Hunslet was the referee,and the teams lined out as follows Huddersfield: Beadyhough, full hack; L;Deere W Dew- hirst, Larard and Kitchen, three.guarters ; A Marslen and F Dowlairst, half-backs; Conley,E V Chambers.F H Chambers, Littlewood, Knight, Furness, ;Naylor and Sutclif a (captain), forwards. .

Warrington : Hallam, full back; Fish, Isherwood, Dicken- son and Harris. three-quarters ; Allen and Burgess half backs; Boardman. Morrison, ICdmoudaon, Fell, Eden, Ash- ton, Taylor and J Swift. forwards. Sutcliffe started for Huddersfield with a light breeze in his favour and play was at once (wriest into Warrington quar- ters. Deere received a pass but was held, by Fish. Then Burgess broke away finely from the scrum, he and Fish running finely to the line, though the wing man was too much obstructed to get through. Huddersfield soon cleared. Kitchen running strongly into the home half where he was grassed by Hallam. Warrington attacked again. Burgess and Harris menacing the Huddersfield line. In tact the lat- ter would have scored but for taking the final pass forward. Warrington were showing the most attractive f o Abell, and Morrifion had hard lines in not leading to something tangible after a splendiairun. Good dribbling by Fell,Edmondson,acd Swift found Huddersfield napping. Dewhirst, however, just gathered the ball in time. Burgess put in some smart work, and almost got past. He now came under the censure of the touch judge, but after a long lecture by Mr Itenton.the referee, he was allowed to re-enter the field. A moment , later the same touch judge stopped the game in consequence of the rude remarks of the spectators.whom his really strange decision had irritated, and the referee had to threaten him with his displeasure. The game proceeded after the incident. and the play was chiefly at centre. Once more the referee had to warn the spectators, and it was perhaps fortunate the whistle blew soon afterwards for half-time, and the obnoxi- ous touch judge croased to the opposite side of the field. The score sheet was clear at the interval. Swift restarted and Warrington at once entered the visitors' half, Burgess brake away grandly and passed to Harris, who scored a minute from the start Fish landed a goal amid loud cheering. A brief visit was paid into the Warrington lhalf:by Huddersfield after this reverse, but Harris dashed uff, and got to the quarter flag, then Fish dribbled over the line, and would certainly have scored a try had he not been deliberately pulled back by Kitchen. ia penalty kick was awarded, but Hallam failed to get a goal. Play continued interesting, and Hudderafleld were playing into a very advantageous position, some dan- gerous passing movements being made byithe Yorkshire three quarters' A long attack was brought to a close by an 1113131.10- ces sful drop at goal. Huddersfield were however attacking again as they had a very fast Bet of threeqoarters, and Larard made a very dangerous dribble. Warrington only just savings try in the nick of time. A fine forward drloble came as a welcome relief to Warrington. and half way was reached. Burgess had a grand chance of opening out the game by the resulting scrimmage, but threw for ward and spoiled the chance. The home threequarters again showed up well and Harris was the means of again giving Warring' ton a distinct advantage. Later in the game Burgess was hurt,and had to leave ths. ficld,Fiah taking the half Sack.and Eden the three-quarters. He eventually resumed nod play was of an,even character..

Final.—Warrington, 5 points ; Huddersfield nil- RUNCORN V, BROUGHTON RANGERS.

The first meeting of these teams in the present season was fixed for Saturday at Runcorn, but owing to the severe frost which had set in the ground was unfit for play, and the match had to be cancelled. _

- SALFORD V SWiNToiv. Salford opened their new ground at Weaste on Saturday and played a match against Swinton. About 17,000 specta- tors were present. Both clubs played their strongest team and a vigorously contested game took place in which Salford managed to secure a lead in the later stages of the game] and kept it to the finish. The play on the whole Was very I disappointing, and neither sets of backs showed anything like their true form. In the first half Swinton had a little more of the play than Salford, and Davies and Morgan gave their three-quarters plenty of opportunifiss, but before they could get going properly the Salford three-quarters had nipped in aptl broke up their combination. The same kind of thing happened when Salford got possession. and the game ms rged into one of strung tackling and scrummages. When half-time arrived there was no score, and it was only towards the finish of the game that Lomas managed to drop a goal from his second attempt which won the match. Even after this reverse Swinton had a splendid opportunity to moss the Salford line, butlValentine threw It away by trying to break through himself itastead of passing out to biampson, who had no opposition. Although Swinton tried desperately to get on level terms they were not successful and the game ended ia a win for Salford by two points to nothing.

A STAGE DUEL

Results in a County Court Action for Injuries at Warrington.

At the Warrington County C.'.; net on Thursday, last werk before his Honour Jud go Bowen Rowlands. K.C., Anna Do Grev,an actress,broueht enaction against Marie Dagmar, proprietress of " A Life's Revenge" Company, for £5O, damages for personal injuries. Mr Browne appeared for the plaintiff, and defendant ',was repre- sented by Mr Glover. of Smethwick. Mr Browne:said plaintiff was engaged to take the part of Marcia Vittoria (a Red Angel) in "A Life's Revenge,'' a play which was presented at the Royal Court :Theatre, Warrington, during the week com- mencing September 30. The chief scene was one in which plaintiff and Miss Dagmar fought a duel with swords. On the first night the duel passed off ao well that the performers were recalled. Oh Tuesday night for some reason or other that they could not find out or imagine, the duel was a terrible affair. In the fight Miss Dagmar had to disarm the plait tiff, throw away her sword, take hold of the plaintiff by the throat and force her to her knees. On Tuesday night, instead of doing this, it was alleged that Miss Dagmar not only forced plaintiff to her knees, but pushed her head heck until it teuched the staee. Plaintiff called out " You to hurting tne,7 but defendant took no notice and con- tinued to shake her. She subsequently dragged plaintiff to her feet, and threw her forward, causing her to fall net the stage. Plaintiff felt vets' great pain internally, but she managed to get up again and re- sume her sword. She was, however, unable to con- thane the duel, as " eveilthiog around her was swimming," and she at length threw down her sword and fell on the sta.:T. The curtain was rung down, and there was uproarious applause, as the people thought it was good Wing, whereas it was acting in reality. The audience accorded a recall, but defendant refused to go on the stage with the plaintiff. Plaintiff was taken to her dressine room, but ultimately resumed h-r perform- ance* She suffered great pain during the night, but she again went through the duel scene on Wednes,lay. That night nothing extraordinary occurred, and plaintiff was complimented oa her acting by Miss Dagmar. Plaintiff also played or Thursday and Friday evenings, but on Friday she was taken ill, and Dr Peacocke had to be called in. Plaintiff was ill for a fortnighe, and had suffered great ag,oriy. Continuing Mr Browne said they all knew it woul'i be said that if an actress took a part, she took it with all he risks. Granted with all ordinary rills. But sup- osing an actress had to be shot at and the other person n a passion or otherwise put shot in the gun, that was not a risk she would undertake in signing the cent, ace for the engagement. So it was in this case Plaintiff en- gaged to go through the duel merle but en!), as it was played at the rehearsals and on Menday night. She nevereugaeed for the risk of being put on her knees and her head bent back till it bemped the floor. That risk was never dreamt of and the duel on Tuesday even- ing was fl most extraordinary and cruel thing. naPaintiff gave evidence in support of th'is statement. She said that during the first round of the duel defend- ant was talking to herself, and said that if She got hold of her (p'aintiff) she would put her down. She seemed to be in a great passion. In cross-,xamination plaintiff denied that on Moaday, Tuesday, Wednesday, andahursday evenings her fight- ing was "very feebly done." She was not " fighting the air." She did not complain to the stage manager of the affair. Mr Henry Houghton, a property man at the theatre corroborated. Dr Peacocke havingleiven evidence as to plaintiff's injuries the case was adjourned until Saturday, Janu- ary 9let.

WARRINGTON.

The Sale of Work at Fairfield Schools.

The children's sale of work in aid of the purchase of school furniture for Fairfield Schools was re-opened on Saturday afternoon by Mrs J Buckton. The room was prettily decorated and the stalls were well filled with useful and ornamental articles.—ln declaring the sale open, Mrs Buckton said that that effort on the part of the girls to help themselves was worthy of their warm support7and encouragement.. They heard a great deal now about free education, and she was inclined to think that parents were apt to forget their responsibili- ties sometimes and to leave the cost of the educa- tion of their children to others more :than they should do. She thought that the effort of the girls of the school and their parents for they of course were going to buy a great deal of what was there was most praiseworthy. The teachers from Miss Taylor downwards deserved the greatest praise, , Toey might say they had done quite enough in gaining such an excellent report from the Inspector but to this they had added their help in preparing that 03ale. Shel hoped the efforts of teachers and girls would be crowned with success and a very satisfactory result obtained.— The Rev AS Cameron movel a hearty vote of thanks to Mrs Buckton for bet kindness in coming and stated that Mrs Buckton was quite the most suitable person to open a sale of work at Fairfield, not only because she was so eloquent a:speak er,but also because Mr Buckton waQ quite the most im- portant person connected with the school, giving as treasurer a great deal of time and trouble to the work. The managers also wished to exprecs their appreciation of the enthusiasm and energy dis- played by Miss Taylor and her staff in working up the side cf work. The ditliculty of mainraininz Voluntary school.:in a state of efficiency was always great, but had others been es enthusiastic and as go-ahead as the teachers of the Fairfield Higher Grade Girls' school there never would have been any difficulty. The vote of thanks was carried with acclamation.—Mr. Buckton briefly responded —The total proceeds, which were in aid of the purchase of school furniture, amounted to S5O.
