THE EXAMINER, FRIDAY DECEMBER 27, 1901 LIBERAL WORKERS.

am concerned it never was my ambition to creep into parliament by methods of that kind. (Hear, hear.) If you had chosen to send me, trusting me to represent the best interests of the working class, and indeed all classes in the town I should have felt highly honoured, but on any other terms I would much rather not be chosen. (Hear, hear.) You are good enough to overlook those shortcomings and in spite of these great difficulties we polled within 26 of the record vote on our side. In that 1 say you did amazingly well, and it is chiefly the most intelligent and capable part of the working class that we have to thank for that result.t4Of the capitalists and large empolyers in our neighbourhood I am sorry to say, there are but few nostadays who give us a backing. It ought not to be so (hear, hear) ; it used not to be so once upon a time. (Hear, hear,) Lord Rosebery—(loud cheera)—has de- precated that condition of things which he has described as ranging all the" Haves" on one side and all the" Have nots " on the other. (Hear, hear.) Still if it is to be so here we must face the facts, and as things stand it seems to me the fortunes and the future of the Liberal Party in Warriugton are really in the bands of the elite of the working class themselves. We must face the situation, and if I may say so, with all respect, I think if you will soberly consider what these reforms ahead of us mean, noe to the lucky moneyed few but to the whole nation, these educational reforms,these practical and feasible temperance reforms this housing of the poor, and this allied question of the taxation ct land values—(cheers)—if you really con- sider the immense practical benefit that they mean to the workers of this country I don't think that as a great working class constituency Warrington will be satisfied to go on for ever taking what is done for us by hard working Liberals in other parts of the country, and fail to take part in the work themselves. (Cheers). - LORD ROSEBERY'S SPERM. . . _

I know we have been divided oci the; question of the , war. To a certain extent there have bran differences, , but these differencea have been chiefly in respect to past 1 events, and I do think within the last week there has been a very significant change in the political atmosphere, and there is no doubt the brilliant speech to which I had the pleasure ot listening at Chesterfield— (cheere)-13as helped very largely to clear the air and we are very much nearer unity than we have been for many a long day. (Laughter.) Let me mention Some of the main points on which we Liberals are all united. First of all we intend now, as we have intended from the first, te give our army all the baekine we can. (Hear, hear, and cheers ) We admire the way in whico our army has fought this wearing, trying, protracted struggle. %Yea:sympathise with them and we admire their courage, their tenacity and their work, (Cheers.) And I know lam speaking to every man here when I say that we know very well Tommy, Atkins is one of the best of good lellows—(cheers)—and he and our army as a whole have fought this fight with all the kindliness and all the chivalry that is worthy of English people— (claeers)—and I will add,with all rho humanity that has been possible in view of the policy it has been their duty to carry out. (Bear, heat). I think I speak for you all here when I say I know we consider one of several colossal blunders and fatal mictakes which this govern- ment has made has been their demand for unconditional surrender—(near, hea.ro—terms teat you or I in the same pcsition would never listen to, terms that ought never to have been named. (Hear, hear). After the capture of Bloemfontein and the fall of Pretoria when we had an opportunity to make terms, when these people were crestfallen 3nd beaten—but not des- perate—(hear,hear)—then was the time for a true state-- ma n a genuine diplomatist, to build the bridge over which a brave toe might • have climbed down. (Cheers). Then another point which you wll find emphatically laid down in Lord Posebery's speech he empbasises both the need for conceding large and liberal terms of amnesty when peace is made and his willingness to make terms. (Cheers). [paw negotiations can best he opened up is a question. But any man who looks to the future must see that for our own sake as well as for the sake of those ill-fated people it would be tar better, if possible, that terms to which they were an assenting party —(hear, bear)—terms based on the incorperation of these countries in the Empire—(hear, hear,)--should be made, than that the struggle should be fought to the bitter end and finally develop into a case of saigner an bianc. Well I think, therefore that al cf us, both those who accept Lord Rosebery's speech in toto and those who accept, if not all, the greater part of it will wel- come this speech of his. We welcome it on more grounds than one. In the first place the purport and tendency of the speech is in the main tbe highest possible tribute to the courage and sagacity with which in his respons- ible position Sir LI Carapbell-Bannerman—(cheem—has fought a most difficult battle for Liberal principles. (Applause). In the second place,and naturally following from this the speech makes for unity in our party, and most important of all,fcr peace in South Africa. (Cheers.) TUE LARGER PATRIOTISM. have only one more topic to mention before I sit down, and that is in reference to Lord Ressbery him- self. I for one think we owe him a considerable debt for a great achievement of bis during the last ten or twenty years. If he has done nothing else he has done a great deal in helping to spread the gospel of what I like Sc call the Lareor Patriotism. (Hear, bear, and cheers.) The opinion that obtains to-day was not always the average opinion of the country. There was a time when good friends of ours of tire Manchester School looked upon the development of self-government among our colonies as tending in the direction of separa- tam. And this opinion was be no means confined to this, that ;or the other section in polities, because you had that eminent statesman Lord Beaconsfield saying that the " Colonies were a millstone round cure neck to be got rid of" (" Oh, oh.") All that is changed now and among the many forces and men wbo have helped to bring about this change during the last two decides, I venture Is say there is; no one to whost the country owes more in that respect, yes, and from whom the country looks for more yaluatle public service, in the future than Lord Rosebery. (Cneers.) Thanks to the brilliant and gifted statesman we do appreciate I believe as a nation, the magnitude of the legacy and the heritage of this great Empire which has been handed down to Us whether as politicians to consolidate and to develop or as soldiers to strengthen and defend. (Cheers.) I was talking some time since to a very able man iu America, Bishop Potter, who had just, when I met him, returned from a tour round the world, and I asked him what had struck him most in that trip. Bishop Potter said, "What struck ma most is the way in which ene hundred thousand of yon English people are able to keep in order and to govern three hundred millions of Orientals in India and tilt! erkehdistto me, there is no parahel ory of the world, and all thing arellmtha for B ur suchm a b a" the world knows we govern these dumb nana„, not merely to this country's advantage, but to the advan- tage, of those poverty stricken countries themselves* (Hear, bear.) That is a stirring fact for us 'all to think of. (Cheers.) SANE IMPERIALISM.

There are prints in the now Imperialism with which I for one have no sympathy. Ido not like things like the Jameson Raid—(hear,hear and loud cries of 'No')— and I do not think—it you puG it on no higher ground. I do not think it is good policy for us to griand white- wash the chief authors of a }tali like that, still less to take them to our Universities and heap honours upon them. (Ilear, bear.) And I have no belief whatever in the New Brummagem diplomacy. (Rear, bear and laughter)—that preceded the war, neither hive I any sympathy with a good part of the government's nolicy that has been in force since the war unfortunately began, but,and this is an important point,' am not going to let them fasten upon me, and if I avore you I would not let them fasten on you eithev, that name of Little Englander because you take that view. (Applause.) We have as much pride in our Empire as any of them have, but all the same it is not Jmgoisrn. It is Imperial- ism, hut it is sane Imporialiam, Ind holding those views I for one think the country owes Lord Resebery 3 °Teat debt for the fine spirit with aihich be has developed these sentiments of a larger patlictisin throughout our nation. I think his return to ad.',ve politics has helped greatly to clear the ground, lane that we shall before very long see Liberals and Liberal imporialiga of all ranks working together fr the good of our common ' cause. I give you gentleman, " Success to that cause in Warrington" which I will coeple with the names of ' Councillor Tinnion and Councillor Evans. (Applause.) The tnaot %Val then drunk with enthusiasm.

It seems to me that it is a most unfortunate tiling that ws should have such pplendid material as we have lafte, and use I.; so badly. Supper at Parr Hall. Address by Mr. A. H. Crosfield.

Mr A. H. Crosfleld entertained at the Parr Hall, Warrington, on Saturday evening, some four hun- dred of those who most actively supported him at the last Parliamentary election in Warrington. The hall was gaily decorated with flags and bunt- ing. An excellent hot-pot supper was served by the Winmarleigh Café Compans to which full jus- tice was done.:Messrs Crostield's Brass Band played selections during supper. Amongst those present were Mr A H Crostield, Alderman Shaw Green, Councillors F R Roberts, William Roberts, Plump- ton, Tinnion, Peacocke, Ellison, Wilkinson, James Evans, Messrs T Birchall, J W Hayward, .T.P., William Furness, J Podmore, C Jackson Holmes, J Mountield, J Clayton (secretary.) After the removal of the cloth, several toasts were proposed and duly honoured.The proceedings throughout were of a most enthusiastic character and an interesting programme of music in various forms was much appreciated. The musical pro- gramme was as follows :—Song, " Mary of Argyle," Mr A Griffiths ; song, " True till death, Mr Berry ; trombone solo, "Village Blacksmith," Mr E Smith ; duet," Excelsior," Messrs W H Ireland and E Pearce; whistling solo,"Il Bacio," Mr W Whittaker; ventriloquial selections, Mr A Grounds ; Lancashire sketch, Mr E A Sanders ; selection " Here's health unto His Majesty," Apollo Male Voice Choir (con- ductor, Mr T 13rough); comic song, Mr Grounds. All the items were exceptionally well given, and credit is due to Mr W H Payton, Mus Bac, for arranging such an excellent programme. THE FIRST TOAST.

MR S WOODS AND THE NEWTON DIVISION.

A meeting of Labour leaders was held on Mon- day night at Haydock in support of the candidature of Mr Sam Woods for the Newton Division of South-west Lancashire. Mr Twist proposed a resolution expressing the opinion that Mr Woods was a fit and proper person to represent the cause of Labour in Parliament, and promising to use every effort to secure his return irrespective of party or creed.—Mr Reed seconded the rtsolution, which was unanimously carried.—Mr Sam Woods said he had just Coale from a meeting at Garswood where similar reaolutions had been Passed. Hitherto be had always stood a; a Labour-Liberal canditure, because it was no use having three-cornered fights; but while there was no one else in the field he had a porfect right to come out as he did. Nobody could dispute his sympathies with the working classes. Under the new arrangement of the Miners' Federation he came out as a Labour candidate, and he had been largely promised the support of the Cons3rvative working men. He was deter- mined to press forward his candidature in the terms of the resolution, and if they would only work together, and get all ideas of sections and fads out of their minds, and with a good register and organization they would win the division.

Presentation to Active Service Volun-

teers at Runcorn.

I A most interesting gatheving was held on Monday evening at the Runcorn Drill Hall, the occasion being the presentation to Privates Chadwick and Timmis, I Active service volunteers, of the South African war I medal and the silver watch and chain subscribed by the townsfolk. The inclement weather interfered with the attendance but the spirit of the audience indicated that feeling stilt ran high with the patriotism shown by the volunteers. Mr R a Posnett, chairman of the Urban Council, occupied the chair and he was supported by Captain Ashton and Captain Timmins. In • few well- chosen words Captain Ashton presented the recipients with the war medals amidst a burst of applaule,remark- ing that they had been sent to him from Chester. He wished that the honour had fallen into more capable hands.He was proud ofthe men and hoped they wouldlibe successful in their future careers.—Mr Ii H Posnett, as representing the inhabitants of Runcorn also struck a happy vein when he said that the time for words was over. They wanted deeds not words in order to finish the war sobich was costing so much blood and treasure He was genuinely pleased to present the watches to Privates Chadwick and Timmins.—Votes of thanks con- cluded a meeting btimful of patriotic feryour.

NOTES ON UAMES AND ATHLETICS RUGBY FOOTBALL.

• By." Ovalist."

_ One of the few matches played last Saturday was the one at Wilderspool between Warrington and Huddersfield. The whole country was frost bound and play was possible on very few football grounds. Only three fixtures under the Northern League were ful- filled, the other two being at Salford and Hull, where the grounds had been carefully pro- tected against the frost. At Warrington there had been no protection at all beyond nature's bounteous covering of thick turf, and it was rather remarkable that play: was at all possible. With the exception of a patch in the centre the surface still had plenty of spring in it and the home team were all anxious for play. Huddersfield arrived half-an-hour late and were for sometime doubtful about playing on the frozen surface but eventually they decided by a majority of the players to risk life, limb, and points.

There was net a very large crowd, many, of the usual enthusiasts concluding that as there *lta skating at various places there could hardly be football at Wilderspool. Still there would be between 3,000 and 4,000 present, which was not bad considering all the circumstances. Both sides were well represented and a fast game was witnessed. Huddersfield are one of the few Yorkshire teams which go in for quick passing, and in their style of play they much resembled War- irington. The homesters were rather the more adept in the art of combination and in the first half they had very hard luck on• at least two occasions in not scoring. Fish and Harris both got within jumping distance of the line, but were not allowed to jump. Huddersfield also made some good runs but up to the interval there had been no score.

Starting the second half with great dash Warrington soon got well into the visitors' half. Burgess broke clear away from a scrimmage at the 25 line and running to the line passed to Harris who scored easily near the posts. Fish landed a goal amid cheers from the pleased partisans of the home club. From now to the close it was a fairly level struggle, the Fartowners making several gallant attempts to get through, Deere and Kitchen both being stopped near the line. War- rington should also have had another try as Fish dribbled over and was deliberately held back with the line at his mercy. The referee, Mr. Renton, only awarded a penalty kick, which came to naught. War- rington maintained their lead of 5 points to the close.

Hallam deserves most praise on the winning side, for he played a really fine game right through, his vis a-vis Readyhough being little inferior. Isherwood and , Harris, Allen and Burgess also did well. Dickenson and Fish were hardly at their best, but running was certainly a matter of some difficulty on the frozen turf. I must also compliment the Warrington forwards on a vastly improved display. Deere was the most dangerous man in the Huddersfield team, but lie was well looked after by Fish. At three-quarter Hudders- field should give a good account of themselves before the season closes. They possess a fast quartette in Deere, Dewhirst, Larard and Kitchen. The halves are only moderate.

The Christmas Day match was to a great extent spoiled by the weather, which was of the vilest description during the morning and none too pleasant afterwards. Water had settled on the ground in huge pools, and football was often farcical in the extreme. It greatly pleased the crowd to see some unfortunate individual in shirt and pants go sailing through the muddy mixture after a vigorous tackle. Both Leigh and Warrington began rather gingerly, but when once they warmed to their work the mud was forgotten and football earnestly played under difficultifs. Warring- ton were soon seen to be the best side and by smart play had credited themselves with a goal and a try to a goal at halftime. Another try was put on in the second half and Leigh failing to score again were beaten by 8 points to 2 points.

The game was by no means uninteresting to watch. Apart from the laughable incidents associated with it, there was plently of good football shown,though every- thing was against combined movements. Every inch of the ground was well contested, and Warrington deservedly had the advantage as a general thing. Burgess played a magnificent game and had a hand in every point scored. He began by outwitting the Leigh halves and giving Harris a chance which the speedy right wing made the most of. Then he dropped a goal in response to one from the foot of Dunbavin, while in the second half he scored a try with a fine single-handed effort. All the time he worked—and looked—like a nigger, and must be con- gratulated on one of the finest displays he has yet given. Townson gave him capable assistance and filled Allen's place with credit. It was thought that Isher- wood would be badly missed, but Hockenhall rose to to the occasion and did very well in the centre position. Hallam wee again tip top and saved Warrineton on many an occasion when a score looked probable. fielding of the wet ball was remarkably fine.

Leigh were quite the inferior side, their combination contrasting. poorly with Warrington's. Dunbavin was the best of the lot, and did a lot of very fine work for his side, while Davies and Kight, the two wings, were next in the order of merit. Forward the teams were well-matched, Warrington quite holding their own against a heavier eight. Altogether the result of the game was very satisfactory to Warrington people, for a rather severe defeat at Leigh was avenged.

Warrington's triumph over Runcorn on Thursday was a source of ecstatic joy to thousands of their sup- porters. That they might win was generally admitted. but the great majority of the 1500 people who followed them hoped for rather than expected such a result,even though the Linnets had had the worst of an encounter with tVidnes the previous day. An immense crowd assembled, the match being the only one in the dis- trict, and I am glad the coffers of the Runcorn club were so well replenished. There must have been 7,000 people round the enclosure at the lowest estimate, and their sympathies appeared to be evenly distributed, judging by the enthusiastic cheers each side got when schue exceptional tit-bit was provided.

I must repeat what has been said before this season. Runcorn are not the Runcorn of old. The defence is as solid as ever, but the attack has woefully diminished in efficiency. The Linnets have changed their style of play to their own disadvantage. Formerly the bicks used to combine with!beautiful accuracy, and supple- ment the work of the forwards by scoring tries. Now i they appear to think that kicking s better than passing and as a result a passing run is seldom seen, and the ultimate outcome is failure. Kick and rush is old- fashioned and well nigh obsolete, and I really am sur- prised to see Runcorn resorting to such a poor style oi play. They have the material for better things, and if they desire to win they must play scientific football the football they used to be famed for.

• Runcorn School Board Election. Answer to the "Church Times." ante Sir—The "Church Times "has published a most mendacious and slanderous attack upon the Progressive party and their policy at the recent School Board election. The IChurchtTimes " says "The so-called Progressives have refrained from no vile charge or epithet to win their way. This is a very sweeping charge. What is there to support it? The only evidence in tha article is a distorted picture of the Boden—firth controversy which is made to appear the chief ground upon which the election was fought. The meetings of the Progres- siva party and the Church party were reported fairly fully. I cannot find in them one reference by the Pro- gressive candidates or their supporters to the incense and absolution question ; but I do find one statement which drew forth Mr Baden's detailed accusations and which, by the way, has not yet been answered. I have in my posgession about 40 handbills published by both sides. There is only one that refers to the Bode° business. And this is not issue Iby the Progressives. It is a bill headed "Incense " and states " I have never either in public or private ministrations burnt: or used! incense. Any statement to such effect is an absolute falsehood." Then follows the signature (Reverend) Id B Firth, M.A." (Why not " priest " on this occasion ?). As a matter of fact the Progreg- sive candidates refused to have anything to do with the Firth question. From the beginning they asserted that whatever bearing the incident might have upon a highly objectionable system of proslytieing it had nothing to . do with the election. The speeches and handbills show distinctly that the election was fought chiefly upsu firures which had been used by Canon Wood arid his colleagues to exalt de- nominational schools and discredit Board Schools. Rasult canon Wood was compelled to publicly acknowledge these figures to be wrong. They show that: even after this acknowledgement on the very morning of the polling day, the church candidates repeated the falsehood in a handbill. They show that the Canon's phrase " strategy of truth" was acted up to by the &lurch party at the last election an at the election ot 1898, and that the progressives revealed the meaning of such a policy in all its naked ugliness. They show that Mr. Posnett had to repel a calumny promulgated by handbills having the names of the Cnurch candidates at the foot. They show that the church party felt that the facts recorded against thews by the Progressives were shaking the ground beneath their lest, as it were, and they were reduced to the expedient of blowing their own trumpet be' describing their own Jesuitical replies as " grand 'r " splendid and the like, as though for fear that the ratepayers would apply some other adjective. The Church party repeatect!their old tactics on throwing dust in the eyes of the people, t ley did not succeed, and now with all the venom and bitterness engendered of defeat, make the" Church Times" the medium of a sweeping charge without an atom of evidence to support. it. It the re- cords of the election establish one thing more clearly than another it is that the proeressivea entered the con- test with clean hands ; that they placed oducatioa far above mere parochial politics aed petty local jealougies, and their magnific-,nt victory is not besmirched by one foul stain. Were the Church pasty's appeal to the higher instincts ot the people Their own speeches and hsndbills reply trumpet toned " no." In their anxiety to retain their falsely won position ' in order to sustain their own selfish interests, they attempted to excite the cupidity of the people by drawing frightful pictures of the cost of Progressive education. Mr Hall Caine might have just gone through the Runcorn election when he made his speech at Manchester a fortnight rago.Never.he said, had the Church been in the forefront in emancipating the people from slavery of brain or muscle. And the attitude of the Runcorn Church party made all for their own power by allowing things to remain in their pre- sent chaotic and unsatisfactory state, and never one step towards that time so freely advocated by the Pro- gressives, when every child shall have the opportunity of a free, wide end generous education for its own sake and for the sake of the nation. The " Church Times need have no concern because the education of the young people of Runcorn is entrusted to a Progressive majority, The religion taught in the 13;ard schools may be without distinction of creed, but the children will be trained in honesty that tears to ohltt..reci arcontitant. truth without prevarication, and honour that will make ansouas for w.og doie.Yo.ears truly, IMPERIALIST.

'COUNTY BOROUGH OF WARRINGTON.- -The Corporation are prepared to Let, from the 2nd February next, all that commodious and well-built HOUSE known as the Old Warps, within the borough, containing four entertaining rooms,six bedrooms.bathroom, w c, butler's pantry, Bm, with the lawn, orchard, 2-stalled stable and two coach-houses. saddle-room, 10, Together with the FARM- HOUSE adjoining, with the stables, sheds, and other out-buildings, and about 22 acres of LAND, including two aeresr,of Twiggery and limited grazing of part of Victoria Park. The farmhouse contains living room and kitchen, with dairy and cellar, three bedrooms and two garrets. There is stabling for three horses,l4 loose boxes and sheds,and accommodation for 12 cows. Permission to inspect the premises can be obtained on application at the Old Warps. Tenders for the yearly tenancy of the whole of the premises, or for the House and Farm separately, to be sent in (endorsed "Tender for Old Warps, or Farm,") on or before the 31st clay of Vecember instant. Dated this 14th day of December, 1901. J. LYON WHITTLE, Town Clerk. Town Hall. Warrington.
